## Research cycle 65 — create a verifiable research snapshot manifest — 2026-10-06

Implemented `src/platform/audit/research-export-manifest.ts` and the `research:manifest` command. A manifest records the SQLite snapshot's SHA-256 and byte length, retained audit row ID/time bounds, event counts, and the persisted research-capture loss marker when its shape is valid. The verifier independently re-hashes the snapshot and re-reads its inventory, detecting snapshot changes and manifest inventory edits. Snapshot hash and inventory are read with before/after file hashes to reject concurrent mutation. `npm run research:manifest -- create <snapshot.sqlite> [manifest.json]` creates a sidecar; `... verify ...` validates it and exits nonzero on failure.

The manifest explicitly reports `completeness: UNKNOWN`, since rows may have been pruned, dropped before persistence, or lost in a crash without a durable marker. A failed-write count does not identify missing row ranges. The manifest is not signed and has no external anchor, so a party able to replace both files can replace the evidence; it provides tamper detection only when the manifest is held independently. SQLite's existing `VACUUM INTO` snapshot API remains the capture mechanism; this adds a verifiable description rather than another backup path. Sources: [SQLite backup API](https://www.sqlite.org/backup.html), [SQLite corruption and backup guidance](https://www.sqlite.org/howtocorrupt.html).

The independent Astra review also found hostile-object allocation edge cases in the isolated block-context helper. The final hardening adds strict input field allowlists, rejects accessors/proxies, manually copies plain JSON data, handles only exact `PublicKey`/BN prototypes, bounds BN/BigInt conversion before string allocation, and validates dense array keys before descriptor materialization. BN word arrays and PublicKey backing values receive the same structural and size checks. Astra's final disposition is acceptable to keep standalone/unwired; it is not accepted as authenticated or canonical evidence and must not be integrated as a truth source.

Verification: TypeScript build passed; focused manifest, joiner, correlator and wiring tests passed 30/30 after the final source edits. Full `npm run test:all` passed core 394, intelligence 541, platform 820, and terminal 285 (2,040 total; zero failures); the last subsequent manifest sidecar-size guard was rebuilt and covered by the focused rerun. `git diff --check`, registry JSON parsing, and CLI usage behavior passed. Export tests cover retained inventory, known-loss reporting, tamper detection, and an actual `Store.backup()`/`VACUUM INTO` snapshot.

Next: add a durable audit-retention/pruning ledger and evidence retry/recovery path so exports can report known missing ID ranges instead of only aggregate failure counts. Then define an independent signing or external anchoring boundary for manifests. Do not claim complete research coverage until those gaps are closed.

## Research cycle 66 — preserve exact audit-pruning gaps in exports — 2026-10-06

The existing `Store.pruneAudit()` deleted rows older than the configured cutoff with no record of which audit IDs disappeared. Added the append-only `audit_prune_ledger` table to the existing Store schema. Pruning now runs under `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`, selects at most 50,000 expired rows, records the precise deleted ID intervals, cutoff, timestamp, row count, time bounds, and compact event-category counts, then deletes exactly those IDs before commit. On failure the transaction rolls back. SQLite documents transaction atomicity and that WAL commits are represented in a separate persistent WAL file, which supports keeping the retention metadata and deletions in the same transaction/database; it does not protect a separately copied bare `.db` file while WAL is active. Sources: [SQLite transactions](https://sqlite.org/lang_transaction.html), [SQLite WAL](https://sqlite.org/wal.html). The returned result contains `limitReached` so a maintenance caller can continue in bounded batches. Event categories intentionally collapse per-signature event suffixes such as `filled:<signature>` to `filled`; payloads and signatures are not copied into the retention ledger.

The research manifest now reports retained audit rows separately from known pruned rows/ranges and their event-category counts. It distinguishes a present but truncated ledger from a legacy snapshot where the ledger table is unavailable. A complete ledger still does not prove capture completeness: failures before SQLite accepted a write, crashes before persisting a loss marker, or direct database tampering remain possible. Overall completeness stays `UNKNOWN`. The ledger is part of the same local SQLite file and is not externally anchored or tamper-proof.

Focused tests exercise bounded API results, transactional exact-range recording across noncontiguous/decreasing ID batches, and a `Store.backup()` snapshot whose manifest verifies the pruned IDs and category counts. Verification: TypeScript build passed; focused manifest/joiner/correlator/wiring suites passed 31/31; full `npm run test:all` passed core 394, intelligence 541, platform 821, and terminal 285 tests (2,041 total; zero failures). The full suite includes the Store migration/lifecycle tests after adding the table. `git diff --check` passed.

Next: add a durable retry/recovery path for failed audit writes, define how recovery reconciles stable IDs without duplicating or rewriting economic state, and externally anchor manifests if researchers need tamper evidence against local database replacement.

## Research cycle 67 — bind runtime-root enum types and state the scenario cohort honestly — 2026-10-06

Independent Astra review found that RuntimeRoot creation strictly validates `cluster` and `transactionVersionSupported`, but the verifier used `String(value)` before enum checks. A hostile object could therefore return an allowed string during coercion and again from `toJSON`, allowing a schema-invalid self-consistent root to verify while running caller hooks. The verifier now requires primitive strings and direct enum membership before hashing. A regression asserts object-valued fields fail with zero `toString`/`toJSON` calls. The separate v1 contract remains unchanged: no schema marker means v1, 16-hex feature digest, historical root hash, and `transactionVersionSupported: all`; newly created v2 roots use a full 64-hex SHA-256 feature digest and bind transaction-version support. The independent re-review confirmed the verifier finding is closed and the old/new hash vectors verify.

The supplied October 5 token-data report also confirms that peak multiples are descriptive, not achievable returns. In its cleaned main cohort, the median observed peak is 1.0899x and 80.36% finish their recorded histories below the first retained price; the report explicitly qualifies short/censored histories, survivorship/composition, selection and non-executable baselines. Its separate chronological price-proxy experiment reports negative means for all four tested rules before its additional cost scenario, and modeled stop triggers do not cap the observed losses. None of these results establishes profitable live trading. The older empirical scenario runner labels its baseline `ALL_TOKENS`, although the parser intentionally excludes records with fewer than two observations and six extreme-peak-review rows. Renamed the rule to `BASELINE_RESEARCH_COHORT`, made the console description explicit, and added source-CSV-record, malformed-width, pre-rule exclusion and included-cohort counts to JSON so the analysis denominator is inspectable. A full streaming pass read 798,431 CSV records and reproduced the 523,351-row clean cohort. Two malformed-width records explain the difference from the report's 798,430 distinct-mint inventory: one short row contains a mint identity and one malformed row has an empty mint. The report's identity count and the scenario tool's raw-record count therefore describe different denominators. The independent `D:\pump\SOL-SYLPH\models\exit-excellence-50` challenger remains a separate deterministic shadow-only experiment, not a production exit policy.

Verification: after rebuilding the engine, focused RuntimeRoot and scenario parser/runner tests passed 17/17 with the legacy hash vector intact. Full `npm run test:all` passed core 395, intelligence 541, platform 822 and terminal 285 tests (2,043 total, zero failures). After the final `sourceRows` to `sourceRecords` terminology clarification, the focused 17/17 tests passed again, the 913 MB source CSV replay completed successfully, and `git diff --check` passed. Exact artifact hashes: `src/platform/truth/runtime-program-root.ts` SHA-256 `8015CEC09C5C3EE72BD8F44D54FBC6A98102ABBD385A5628A2275FB1DA371488`; `test/platform/runtime-root-hash-schema.test.mjs` SHA-256 `D84ABE9965F1BB0134235104EFDD6DADC9BD95A2D66B1924AE62166B453017F4`; `dist/platform/truth/runtime-program-root.js` SHA-256 `275451AEEEFAE7E477AE39C64CCD5C62316A8EB1CFB0DA9C86D4881F8C37B772`. Sources: user-supplied `solana_all_data_report.md` and `solana_deeper_research.md` derived from [the pinned PumpFun corpus](https://huggingface.co/datasets/Slinky21/Pumpfun_Memecoin_Corpus/tree/13bec2bf3089d56d50c35e602616d1b993bb37f5), alongside the delivered November, migration, registry and reported-execution data files; see also [Jupiter Swap API V2 Build](https://developers.jup.ag/docs/swap/build) for the current execution evidence boundary.

Next: close the durable failed-research-write recovery gap with stable event identity, bounded replay, duplicate suppression and crash/disk-full tests, then add an independent signature or external anchor for exported manifests. Keep the experimental block-context joiner and all profitability conclusions outside live authority until their source/chronology requirements are evidenced and independently reviewed.

## Research cycle 68 — reject non-idempotent spool draft and define the recovery boundary — 2026-10-06

Re-read the supplied October 5 report as research input. Its central limits are consistent with the prior CSV replay: 523,351 multi-observation non-extreme records are not 523,351 executable trades; the 1.0899x median is a hindsight maximum; the separate chronological price-proxy rules all had negative mean gross returns; and missing/censored histories and non-executable quotes prevent a profitability claim. The appropriate next engineering target remains capture of complete point-in-time candidates, quote availability/failure, attempted entry/exit, fills, fees and unresolved exits on fresh dates. The scenario backtest denominator terminology and 913 MB main-file replay are recorded in cycle 67; this report adds no evidence that would justify enabling a strategy.

An independent code review of `src/platform/audit/durable-research-spool.ts` found that its current API cannot provide exactly-once audit insertion: replay omits `eventId` when calling `Store.appendAuditEvent()`, so a process crash after SQLite commit but before spool compaction inserts the same observation again on restart. Concurrent replay can dispatch the same head record. Appends are not fsynced before acceptance, torn/corrupt lines are silently discarded, rewrite/unlink failures are swallowed after memory state is advanced, and recovered entries bypass configured bounds and event validation. A partial Engine hook now optionally enqueues rejected writes and exposes spool counters when a caller injects the spool; `drainResearchSpool()` is manual only. The production `new Engine(...)` composition does not construct/pass a spool or schedule replay, and Store still does not receive the event ID for atomic deduplication. Failed spool enqueue results are ignored, so a second loss is not reflected in the capture-loss marker. This partial hook is not durable recovery and must not be presented as such.

The accepted storage contract for a follow-up implementation is narrower and testable: each observation must have a stable ID tied to its source event identity/revision; target persistence must receive that ID and atomically commit the ID plus audit payload in the same SQLite transaction; replay of an already committed identical ID must return success without another audit row, while reuse of an ID with different content must fail closed. The spool may remove an item only after that idempotent commit is acknowledged, and an uncertain acknowledgment must be safe to replay. Enqueue acceptance needs an explicit durability boundary and observable bounded-capacity rejection. Startup recovery must validate the complete record schema and limits, surface corrupt tails rather than silently treating them as absent, and expose pending/rejected/recovery state. Tests must cover crash after target commit before spool cleanup, restart, concurrent drain, torn append, capacity and payload limits, disk-full/compaction failure, and same-ID/different-payload conflict. The existing account-specific `pa2_outbox` is not a substitute because research observations must not mutate paper-account accounting state.

SQLite is already opened with WAL and `synchronous=FULL`; this supports a database-native atomic dedupe/write boundary, but does not decide migration compatibility, audit-retention interaction, or stable identity semantics. In particular, dedupe identity must remain recognized across audit pruning or a delayed spool replay could reinsert a pruned row. Resolve that retention/lifecycle contract before implementation. Sources: [SQLite UPSERT](https://www.sqlite.org/lang_UPSERT.html) documents a unique constraint and `DO NOTHING` path for idempotent insertion; the project’s transaction wrapper is `src/platform/storage/generation-sqlite.ts` and audit persistence lives in `src/db-worker.ts`/`src/store.ts`.

The manifest HMAC API was tightened so a key and signer ID must be supplied together, signer/anchor metadata is bounded and validated, anchor claims are included in the signed digest, and HMAC bytes are compared with `timingSafeEqual`. The CLI now accepts signing and verification keys from `SYLPH_RESEARCH_MANIFEST_SIGNING_KEY`, `SYLPH_RESEARCH_MANIFEST_SIGNER_ID`, and `SYLPH_RESEARCH_MANIFEST_VERIFICATION_KEY` environment variables, keeping key material out of command arguments and output; unsigned behavior remains the default. `SYLPH_RESEARCH_MANIFEST_WITNESS_ROOT_SHA256` and `SYLPH_RESEARCH_MANIFEST_ANCHOR_REFERENCE` attach a signed witness claim. Focused tests cover API signing, wrong/missing keys, edited anchor metadata, partial configuration, malformed anchors and CLI sign/verify behavior. An `EXTERNAL_WITNESS_ROOT` remains signed claim metadata, not independently checked by the verifier; key custody and actual publication to an independent witness remain external requirements. Do not describe HMAC as external anchoring.

Follow-up after the initial review: the Engine now derives a stable SHA-256 event ID from event type, event name, source record ID and serialized payload, and passes that same ID to both the initial Store append and replay. Store writes the audit row and an ID/content-hash dedupe marker in one `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction. Replaying the same ID and content returns the original audit ID without inserting; reusing an ID with different content fails closed. The marker is retained when its audit row is pruned, preventing a late replay from resurrecting the row. Focused Store tests cover exact replay, conflicting content and prune/replay; the Engine test confirms first-write and replay IDs match. Enqueue failures now increment the Engine's research-loss counter. The independent reviewer confirms these changes close the duplicate-insert finding for the production Store path.

The spool remains a prototype, not an accepted crash-durable recovery service. Production composition still constructs Engine without a spool and schedules no startup/periodic/shutdown drain. Appends do not fsync the spool file or parent directory; recovery silently skips malformed/torn lines and does not validate complete recovered-record bounds/schema; concurrent replay is not serialized. Compaction/unlink failure is recorded only in an in-memory counter after pending memory state has already advanced, so a crash can lose the only replay copy. The spool's drained-ID index is process-local, but the target Store supplies durable audit-event dedupe markers and, as of cycle 76, journal rows use content-addressed immutable IDs enforced by the journal tables. SQLite audit dedupe markers are retained indefinitely, creating unbounded database growth. Custom Store implementations may ignore the optional third parameter and therefore do not satisfy the audit idempotency contract. These gaps block production acceptance.

Verification after the follow-up edits: engine build passed; focused spool, Store dedupe/lifecycle, manifest, and settlement tests passed 23/23; `git diff --check` passed. Complete `npm run test:all` passed all 2,054 tests with zero failures (core 395, intelligence 541, platform 833, terminal 285). Next: define a production-owned lifecycle and crash-durable spool protocol, validate or quarantine corrupt records, serialize draining and test compaction-failure recovery, and set a safe durable dedupe-retention policy that cannot re-enable resurrection. Then obtain independent review and run the full suite. HMAC key custody and real external anchoring remain separate requirements.

## Research cycle 69 — revalidate spool changes against the full suite — 2026-10-06

The supplied October 5 token-data report was re-read as research context. Its new cross-dataset identity union (1,567,676 distinct mint strings after adding reported executions) does not alter the market-performance denominator: it is not a count of independent or complete price histories. The findings remain descriptive: launch-cohort maxima are modest for the typical record, terminal observed values often fall below the first retained price, reported fills are unverified, and the chronological proxy experiment has negative mean returns under all four tested rules before its extra cost scenario. No entry, exit, stop, or token-selection rule is justified by this report. Existing audit notes already capture its key statistical limits and the distinctions between launch, migration, registry, and publisher-reported execution populations.

Validation of the current staged spool/Store/manifest changes first produced one isolated count mismatch in the Engine spool telemetry test (observed 3 versus expected 2). The focused test then passed eight consecutive runs, the complete platform suite passed 835/835, and the subsequent complete `npm run test:all` passed core 396, intelligence 541, platform 835, and terminal 285 tests (2,057 total; zero failures). This run preceded the latest spool edit. This is not evidence that the spool is production-ready; retain the non-reproduced count mismatch as a possible flaky test if it returns.

Official [Node.js fs documentation](https://nodejs.org/download/release/v26.8.2/docs/api/fs.html) documents `flush: true` for synchronous append/write (supported since v20.10.0); the repository runtime gate requires Node 24.15. Spool appends now request a file flush before acceptance, and compaction writes a flushed exclusive temporary file before rename. Recovery checks the file size before reading, rejects malformed/torn records rather than silently omitting them, validates bounded fields, and enforces recovered item and payload limits. This improves file-content durability and visibility of damage; it does not fsync the containing directory, and atomic rename/power-loss semantics still depend on the Windows filesystem. SQLite `synchronous=FULL` likewise syncs the WAL per commit in WAL mode, but does not make a separate spool file part of the same transaction ([SQLite PRAGMA documentation](https://sqlite.org/pragma.html)).

The independent follow-up review accepted the changes conditionally under the documented single-writer constraint. A failed append rolls back to the previously flushed file length and fsyncs that rollback; if rollback fails, the spool latches and rejects future writes. Recovery accepts the earlier `spool_<24 hex>` and caller-supplied event ID formats, bounds legacy error strings, rejects malformed/torn records, and validates recovered limits. Records older than the seven-day retry window remain at the queue head with `SPOOL_RECORD_EXPIRED_HELD`; research evidence is not silently discarded. Append, compaction, replay serialization, legacy compatibility, and age holding have regression tests. The reviewer also found and rejected a proposed finite dedupe TTL because it would permit a held spool record to resurrect an audit row after its marker expired. That TTL change was removed; anti-resurrection markers remain permanent and their database growth is still an open issue.

This remains a single-writer prototype. The code now states that one instance/process must own a spool path, but no interprocess lock enforces this. The reviewer specifically warns that multiple owners can race append rollback or compaction. Directory-entry durability across sudden power loss remains filesystem-dependent. Historical spool records that predate stable audit IDs may duplicate once if an old process had an uncertain commit; no retrospective identity can eliminate that ambiguity. No production composition constructs the spool or schedules startup/periodic/shutdown recovery. Do not wire it until ownership, operator salvage, lifecycle and directory-durability limits are resolved. Journal event identities are now bounded and stable for captured payloads (cycle 76), but production replay compatibility still needs to be verified against the concrete Store and lifecycle.

Final cycle 69 verification after removal of the unsafe dedupe-TTL patch and legacy-error bound: `npm run build:engine` passed; focused spool/Store/lifecycle tests passed 18/18; `git diff --check` passed; complete `npm run test:all` passed core 396, intelligence 541, platform 839 and terminal 285 (2,061 total, zero failures). A prior single telemetry count mismatch did not reproduce in eight focused reruns or the subsequent full runs; retain it as a possible test flake if it returns. The later cycle 70 tests add five focused cases and their full-suite totals are recorded below.

## Research cycle 70 — separate observed token peaks from executable outcomes — 2026-10-06

Read the complete user-supplied October 5 report, including its new November, migration, publisher-execution, and timing/censoring analyses. The report's identity union across five data CSVs is 1,567,676 mint strings, not a denominator of complete, independent price histories. The principal launch cohort remains 523,351 multi-observation histories after the declared exclusions; its median hindsight peak is 1.0899x, 80.36% of recorded endpoints are below the first retained price, and only 0.397% show a 10x peak. The additional cohorts are not interchangeable: the November sample has 8.96% observed 2x peaks but 86.71% of its eventual 2x group ends below its initial recorded price; the selected post-migration sample has 41.76% observed 2x peaks and 9.78% observed 10x peaks, while 72.29% of all its endpoints and 54.98% of its 2x group end below baseline. Publisher-reported fills have no independently reconstructable signatures/wallets; July's median reported return is -68.55%, and 30.95% report losses of at least 90%. These are distinct, source-qualified descriptions, not executable strategy estimates. Main-cohort histories are usually short, temporal comparisons are confounded by composition and sampling, future peak/survival fields are unavailable at entry, and the four price-proxy rules previously tested remain negative on average before added costs. No new strategy, stop, market-wide loss rate, or trading threshold is justified.

Comparing those findings with current code found an evidence-labeling defect in `EconomicFreshnessEngine`: it called `observedPeakMultiple * (1 - simulatedTradeSizeSol / quoteReservesSol)` an `executablePeakMultiple`, capped that heuristic at a 50% haircut, used a fixed 15% fallback with no reserves, and reported empty history as a 1x path with zero impact. This calculation is not an executable quote or a validated AMM model (it omits route/pool semantics, token-side reserves, fees, quote availability, latency, slippage floor, and settlement). The implementation now reports `OBSERVED_PRICE_PATH_ONLY`, keeps `executablePeakMultiple` explicitly `null` with reason `UNAVAILABLE_NO_POINT_IN_TIME_SELL_QUOTE`, and no longer fabricates an impact estimate. Empty histories return null/unknown outcome fields; invalid baselines, tick values, sparse arrays, and backward timestamps reject. Post-peak drop timing now starts strictly after the index of the selected peak, so a same-timestamp lower observation before the peak cannot be miscounted as a post-peak drawdown. Exact observed reserve amounts remain labeled as source tick context, not liquidity guarantees.

Fresh primary-source comparison: current Jupiter Swap API V2 `/build` creates a swap flow from current route/quote information; its documented minimum output and errors are execution constraints, not historical evidence. Solana `getTransaction` supplies a landed transaction by signature (or `null`), including slot and metadata such as fee/error, so it can support reconciliation of landed attempts but cannot reconstruct candidates that never received a quote or transaction. Therefore a credible dataset must preserve each candidate, quote request/result or no-route failure, submission attempt, transaction signature/status, fill amounts, and fees at their observed times, with missing/unresolved outcomes retained. Sources: [Jupiter Swap API V2 Build](https://developers.jup.ag/docs/swap/build), [Solana `getTransaction`](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/http/gettransaction), [Solana RPC method index](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/http).

Verification: nine economic-freshness regressions cover no fabricated executable peak, empty-history unknowns, baseline and tick peak drawdowns, same-timestamp ordering, invalid/nonchronological values, sparse/accessor/inherited/proxy inputs, snapshot semantics, and overflow. `npm run build:engine` passed; focused tests passed 9/9; independent Astra verification found no remaining source blockers; the final full `npm run test:all` passed core 396, intelligence 550, platform 839, and terminal 285 tests (2,070 total, zero failures); `git diff --check` passed. The source remains a research analysis helper and has no identified runtime decision callers. Next highest-value work is a reviewed production-owned flight-recorder capture of candidate→quote→submission→settlement evidence on fresh dates, preserving unavailable/failed paths and verifiable raw evidence. The durable research spool remains unwired until single-writer ownership and salvage are enforced. Do not promote a trading strategy from the historical peak or publisher-reported data.

## Research cycle 71 — make flight-recorder emptiness truthful — 2026-10-06

Source-wide production call-site inspection found `terminal/server.mjs` constructs the SQLite-backed `EconomicFlightRecorder` and exposes its attempt-history GET route, but `recordDiscovery()` and `advanceLifecycle()` have no production callers outside tests. The browser drawer previously promised that candidate execution attempts “will be captured” and rendered an empty database as though it described the absence of attempts. It does not: `Engine.persistResearchObservation()` writes different candidate/gate/paper-simulation audit events to the general audit Store, not this execution flight-recorder database; neither stream alone proves real-chain settlement.

The API now returns an explicit `EMPTY` or `RECORDED` status and revision count. The drawer validates their consistency with the returned revisions and maps malformed/unrecognized responses to `UNKNOWN`; its empty text states only that this database has no recorded revisions, and explicitly says that this cannot establish no candidate/order attempts occurred. Endpoint failures make no completeness claim. This is a UI/API truthfulness fix, not production evidence capture: the recorder remains unwritten by the live execution path, and candidate, rejected/no-route, attempted orders, point-in-time quotes, fills, fees, signatures, and settlement evidence are still not comprehensively connected.

Verification: `npm --prefix terminal test` passed all 286 tests, including new EMPTY/RECORDED and inconsistent-response cases; `npm --prefix terminal run build` passed. The initial test run caught a test path error and an incorrectly specified malformed fixture; both were corrected before the passing run. The terminal build refreshed tracked bundles under `terminal/dist`. Final source/test review confirmed only the recorder declaration (no production callers) and `git diff --check` passed; reported line-ending warnings do not indicate whitespace errors. Next work should design a production-owned capture path around actual Engine/gateway events and durable identity/ordering before wiring the UI to imply lifecycle completeness; retain failed/unavailable/no-route observations and reconcile landed signatures against chain data. No strategy promotion is supported by this change.

Process note: the project charter in [AGENTS.md](../../AGENTS.md) routes UI-only presentation to Sol UI and requires independent Astra review for critical changes. This was a bounded read-only UI/status correction with no execution or economic semantic change, so a separate review gate is not triggered. Any lifecycle-writer integration will be reassessed against the charter before implementation.

## Research cycle 72 — prevent stale flight-recorder rows after invalid refreshes — 2026-10-06

Revalidated the current working tree and public GitHub state before acting: local `main` and `origin/main` both resolve to `373143a10d05327149cc5d3cbd0cfb44bff9cc18`; `git ls-remote` found no new `main` commit. The public repository page currently reports zero issues and zero pull requests. The GitHub CLI is not installed in this environment, so this is a page/remote-ref check rather than a full issues/PR API audit. Relevant prior chats (“Harden SYLPH into production”, “Architect SYLPH FUSION trading”, and earlier trading-bot reviews) consistently caution that research components and paper fills do not prove production integration or on-chain outcomes. The previous cycles on RuntimeRoot v2 and Solana Simulation Evidence remain recorded at cycles 67 and 43–48; neither was reimplemented here.

Comparing that evidence boundary to the current Flight Recorder drawer found that a successful earlier response could remain on screen after a later endpoint failure, JSON error, or malformed record. The component set `UNKNOWN` but retained the prior `attempts`, `history`, and selected row; it therefore rendered stale lifecycle evidence while the newest read was unavailable. The response parser now accepts only rows with a known lifecycle stage, non-empty economic-fact and execution-generation IDs, and non-negative safe-integer revisions, in addition to matching the explicit count/status. Failed, inconsistent, and malformed reads clear prior rows and selection, so the UI shows status unavailable rather than implying stale data is current. Empty status also clears stale selection. This is still a read-only presentation improvement, not a lifecycle writer or completeness guarantee.

Research context: the official Solana `simulateTransaction` and `getTransaction` contracts distinguish simulated execution output from a transaction returned by the ledger with its slot and metadata; the repo’s own `SimulationCertificate` work likewise keeps simulation evidence non-authorizing. This supports the existing project rule that future recorder rows must identify their evidence class and that a paper simulation cannot be displayed as chain settlement. Sources: [Solana `simulateTransaction`](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/http/simulatetransaction), [Solana `getTransaction`](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/http/gettransaction), and [public Sylph Fusion repository](https://github.com/MrPineapples22/sylph-fusion).

Verification: new response tests reject null rows, negative revisions, unknown stages, and empty identities while accepting valid discovery rows. The full terminal suite passes 286/286 after correcting its valid fixture to include the persisted `DISCOVERED` stage; terminal production build passes; `git diff --check` passes. The first test attempt correctly caught the fixture-contract mismatch, then passed after correction. Terminal build output remains tracked and updated. The larger architecture blocker remains: `terminal/server.mjs` constructs a standalone recorder database and has no production connection to the primary Engine; the recorder’s sequential success-only lifecycle also cannot represent gate rejection/no-route/build-abandonment faithfully. A production integration needs architecture review and a reviewed event identity/branch model before being wired. The separate audit Store events currently cover candidate discovery, snapshots, restrictions, entry gates, build results, and explicitly labeled simulated paper fills, but do not establish finalized on-chain settlement.

## Research cycle 73 — make v3 schema preflight a consistent SQLite snapshot — 2026-10-06

The newly supplied October 5 token-data report was read in full and compared with cycles 67–70. Its updated cross-file identity union is 1,567,676 distinct mint strings across the five data CSVs, including the reported-execution file; this is an identity count, not a count of complete histories, independently verified fills, or independent observations. The report's price-peak, censoring, reported-execution, and chronological price-proxy limitations are consistent with the prior audit. It adds no validated profitable strategy, executable edge, or reason to change trading authority. The source cohort and execution-evidence boundaries remain explicit.

Full-suite testing surfaced a race in paper-account v3 schema initialization: the preflight read `PRAGMA user_version` and `sqlite_schema` in separate snapshots. A concurrent worker could commit between them, pairing the old version with the newly installed schema and causing a false `PAPER_ACCOUNT_V3_UNVERSIONED_DATABASE_NOT_EMPTY` rejection. Source inspection now reads version and verifies schema inside one explicit SQLite read transaction. On inspection failure it rolls back; if rollback fails or leaves the handle in a transaction, it closes that handle and reports `PAPER_ACCOUNT_V3_SOURCE_INSPECTION_ROLLBACK_FAILED`. Preflight still runs before WAL configuration, while the mutating initializer rechecks under `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`.

This follows SQLite's documented snapshot isolation and explicit transaction lifetime: [SQLite isolation](https://www.sqlite.org/isolation.html) and [SQLite transactions](https://www.sqlite.org/lang_transaction.html). The documentation also notes that a failed `COMMIT` can leave a transaction active, supporting the close-on-uncertain-rollback behavior. Astra independently reviewed the final compatibility behavior and accepted the diff; existing v0/v1/v2/v3 migration semantics remain covered. RuntimeRoot v2 compatibility rules were also rechecked: new roots use the full 256-bit `activeFeatureSetHash`, strict primitive/list/epoch/context validation prevents JSON coercion of invalid values, and legacy v1 retains its prior 16-hex digest/hash vector and default transaction support `all`.

Verification: final `npm test` passed core 396/396, intelligence 550/550, platform 841/841, and terminal 286/286 (2,073 total; zero failures). Platform coverage includes stable-snapshot inspection, injected commit/rollback failure with poisoned-handle closure, and six concurrent two-worker initialization pairs. `git diff --check` passed. No live trading behavior, provider configuration, or strategy policy changed.

Next: the main unresolved integration gap remains execution-evidence capture. The terminal flight recorder is still not written by the production Engine, while a success-only linear lifecycle cannot faithfully represent rejections, no-route outcomes, build abandonment, retries, and settlement reconciliation. Treat that as an architecture change requiring an explicit event identity/branch model and independent review before wiring; retain missing/unavailable outcomes instead of inferring that an empty recorder means no attempts.

## Research cycle 74 — validate evidence projections and close RuntimeRoot coercion gap — 2026-10-06

Read the supplied October 5 token-outcomes report, its saved statistics and chronological simulation outputs, the earlier “Create detailed Solana token CSV” chat, and the project’s Solana Simulation Evidence work in cycles 43–48. The main priced cohort contains 523,351 multi-observation records after the report’s stated extreme-value exclusions; its median recorded maximum is 1.0899× and 80.36% end their *recorded histories* below the first retained price. The median history spans 90.3 seconds, so these are not end-of-life results or executable entry returns. In the separate PumpSwap snapshot experiment, 1,120 simulated entries were tested under four preselected exits; all four mean gross price-proxy returns were negative, with approximately 62.4 seconds median trigger-to-exit-observation delay. This is sparse snapshot evidence, not Solana transaction latency or a realized-fill backtest. The intended stop did not cap losses in that proxy. None of these findings supplies a validated trading policy or justifies changing live authority.

External protocol research confirms the separation needed in the event evidence: Solana `simulateTransaction` returns simulation output, while `getTransaction` returns a confirmed transaction record (or null); neither an observed peak nor a simulated/paper fill should be labeled as finalized settlement. Jupiter’s current Swap API is v2, with managed `/order` + `/execute` and self-managed `/build` + `/submit` paths. Its current docs distinguish a quote from an assembled transaction and document route/build/landing failures; the older Metis v1 error page cited by the historical report is marked deprecated. These contracts support explicit outcome classes and preserving unavailable or failed entries/exits. They do not justify connecting a new adapter to the current paper-only Engine. Sources: [Solana simulateTransaction](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/http/simulatetransaction), [Solana getTransaction](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/http/gettransaction), [Jupiter Swap API](https://developers.jup.ag/docs/swap), [Jupiter Order & Execute](https://developers.jup.ag/docs/swap/order-and-execute), [Jupiter legacy Metis errors](https://developers.jup.ag/docs/swap/v1/common-errors).

Applied two bounded verification improvements. The Engine Candidate Audit projection now uses `PROJECTABLE`/`UNPROJECTABLE`, requires a minimum event-specific candidate/attempt identity before exposing allowlisted fields, validates that the UI’s invalid-row count matches the returned rows, and enforces payload limits in UTF-8 bytes. This strengthens the read-only panel without claiming full payload validity or completeness. Separately, an independent RuntimeRoot v1/v2 compatibility review found that certificate construction stringified untrusted cluster and transaction-version enum values before strict root verification. The builder now requires primitive strings and checks enum membership directly; a regression proves hostile `toString` hooks are not invoked. Astra Verification accepted the corrected compatibility path. RuntimeRoot v2 still uses a full 256-bit feature-set hash and explicit input validation; v1’s 16-hex digest, historical preimage, and `all` transaction support remain unchanged. These hashes establish structural consistency, not authenticated RPC provenance or proof of actual feature activation.

Current public GitHub `main` remains at `373143a10d05327149cc5d3cbd0cfb44bff9cc18`, matching the local checkout; public issue and pull-request pages show zero open items. The changes remain uncommitted alongside pre-existing worktree modifications. Keep chart peaks and the archived price-proxy results descriptive. Continue architecture review before joining Engine events to a canonical lifecycle or changing any transaction authority.

The project's earlier “Upgrades,” “Sol,” and “SOL_SYLPH” conversations point toward point-in-time entry/exit evidence and careful denominator accounting; those were applied in prior cycles and not repeated as strategy claims.

The independent re-review of the Engine candidate-audit projection found no concrete blocker. It confirms the backend emits `PROJECTABLE`/`UNPROJECTABLE`, applies event-specific identity requirements, clears exposed identity for unprojectable records, and computes the invalid-row count exactly; the UI parser independently enforces those constraints. The SQLite reader enforces the 65,536-byte payload bound in UTF-8 bytes. The panel keeps completeness `UNKNOWN`, distinguishes the retained Engine event projection from the legacy linear flight recorder, and identifies paper fills as simulation rather than settlement. The reviewer clarified that `PROJECTABLE` means allowlisted projection plus minimum identity, not full source schema validation; the label does not claim otherwise.

Re-ran the repository `npm test` pipeline successfully after the review. Exact suite totals: core 396/396, intelligence 550/550, platform 842/842, terminal 292/292 (2,080 passing; zero failures). `git diff --check` passed; Git printed only its existing LF-to-CRLF working-copy notices. No live authority, transaction policy, or profitability rule changed.

The highest-value blocker remains production-owned, restart-safe event recovery. Stable event IDs and SQLite atomic deduplication close the repeated-insert window, but the spool is not instantiated by production composition and has no scheduled lifecycle drain; its documented single-writer constraint is not enforced with an interprocess lock, and manifest completeness correctly remains unknown. Do not wire the spool or the legacy flight recorder as a shortcut. Next, design and independently review an explicit process-ownership and startup/periodic/shutdown recovery contract, including how failures remain visible across restart and how candidate, quote failure, entry, partial/full exit, and unresolved outcomes retain complete denominators. ## Research cycle 76 — enforce immutable journal identity without losing repeated observations — 2026-10-06

The research journals had been vulnerable to silent same-key overwrite: retrying an existing counterfactual/falsification ID with changed JSON updated the JSON column while leaving denormalized query columns stale. The Store now serializes each write with `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`, treats an exact payload plus matching indexed columns as an idempotent retry, rejects changed payload under an existing ID, and detects a corrupted/inconsistent indexed row without replacing it. Direct SQLite corruption regressions cover both journal families.

The first strict implementation exposed a real upstream identity collision: repeated observations in the same opportunity/slot produce different timestamped counterfactuals, and falsification output can also change while mint/slot/thesis status remain constant. IDs are now derived from full SHA-256 digests of the complete report/evaluation payload. Counterfactual IDs are fixed length (`cfr_` plus 64 hex characters), so unbounded caller-provided opportunity IDs cannot exceed the research spool's 128-character identity bound. Replaying the same captured object keeps its ID; changed observations receive distinct IDs. Existing database rows and already queued payloads retain their historical IDs and remain queryable/replayable. External consumers that synthesize `cfr_${opportunityId}_${slot}` must switch to the returned `evaluationId`.

Engine journal error handling now marks immutable identity conflicts as permanent and does not put them into the transient retry spool. `CounterfactualRegretStore` handles both synchronous throws and rejected promises and exposes its process-local failure count and last error through `getJournalHealth()`. It does not persist that health state or surface it in the terminal UI; that remains outside this narrow fix.

The independent Astra recovery reviewer accepted this slice after review of transaction semantics, exact retry compatibility, indexed-row corruption detection, fixed-length ID bounds, and caller error handling. Focused build/tests passed, including the previously failing end-to-end settlement test after collision removal (25/25 before the fixed-length adjustment; 24/24 focused checks after it). The final full `npm test` run passed core 396, intelligence 551, platform 846 and terminal 292 tests (2,085 total, zero failures). `git diff --check` passed. The production file-spool lifecycle/crash-durability gaps described in cycles 68–75 remain open; this change does not connect the spool to production or establish crash-safe recovery.

The focused Store suite passes 8/8 and the engine build passes. ## Research cycle 78 — bound terminal shutdown and capture live source/runtime drift — 2026-10-06

Read-only current-system inspection found a Node process (PID 20520) listening on `127.0.0.1:8793`, launched at 06:22 local on October 6 with relative command `node terminal/server.mjs`. At 10:09 local, `/health` returned HTTP 200 and identified `sylph-paper-terminal` in paper mode; `/api/live/readiness` returned `liveReady:false` and `productionCapitalAuthorityBlocked:true`. `/api/flight-recorder/attempts` returned the legacy `{ok:true, attempts:[]}` shape (zero attempts, no completeness fields), while the current source includes explicit `EMPTY`/`RECORDED` status and a read-only `/api/engine/research-audit` endpoint that the running service returned as 404. The checked-out `terminal/server.mjs` modification time is 09:04 and generated `dist` files are 10:01, later than the process start. Since the process command uses a relative path and its working directory was not observable, this does not prove the listener runs this checkout. Its response is old-contract evidence; the current UI parser must keep treating it as `UNKNOWN`, not as a verified empty recorder. The current-code DB path resolver independently returned `ENGINE_DATABASE_UNAVAILABLE`; the process environment and database path could differ, so this is not attributed to the listener.

Current terminal source created a persistent `SQLiteExecutionAttemptStore`, but its SIGINT/SIGTERM handler stopped periodic/feed services and called `server.close()` without closing that store. Added `terminal/server-shutdown.mjs` to make shutdown idempotent: stop services, stop accepting requests, close idle keep-alives, force-close remaining HTTP connections after a bounded 10-second drain, then close the persistent recorder store. Close/store errors are reported and do not skip cleanup. The server intentionally does not call `process.exit()` in this path. Three tests cover order/idempotency, timeout/error reporting, and invalid configuration.

External runtime research checked Node.js v24.17 primary documentation. `server.close()` stops acceptance and waits for active requests; `closeAllConnections()` forcibly terminates active HTTP(S) connections and should be used cautiously; Windows does not support SIGTERM as a normal signal, while terminal SIGINT is supported. Accordingly, this change bounds handled shutdowns but cannot guarantee cleanup after Windows process termination or machine power loss. Sources: [Node.js HTTP server shutdown methods](https://nodejs.org/download/release/v24.17.0/docs/api/http.html#serverclosecallback), [Node.js process signal behavior](https://nodejs.org/download/release/v24.17.0/docs/api/process.html#signal-events).

This is resource cleanup, not production spool integration or flight-recorder event wiring. The discovered live/source contract drift remains unresolved until the owner confirms or restarts the intended service; no process was restarted or modified. Next audit: determine whether the running paper terminal is intended to serve this checkout, add an explicit build/runtime identity to prevent silent source drift, and continue designing branch-aware capture of rejection, no-quote, abandonment, partial-exit, and unresolved-settlement outcomes.

`node --check terminal/server.mjs` passed; the focused shutdown suite passed 3/3; final `npm test` passed core 396, intelligence 551, platform 847 and terminal 295 tests (2,089 total, zero failures). `git diff --check` passed.

Solana's `simulateTransaction` simulates without broadcasting, and `getTransaction` may return null until confirmed at the requested commitment; therefore neither simulation nor a missing transaction response should be projected as finalized settlement. ## Research cycle 80 — bound runtime artifact identity scans and revalidate RuntimeRoot compatibility — 2026-10-06

Re-read the complete October 5 token-data research report together with the existing Solana Simulation Evidence findings, current RuntimeRoot v2 design, certificate readers/fixtures, and the recent audit history. The report's key results remain descriptive: its cleaned 523,351-token cohort has a 1.0899x median observed maximum and 80.36% below first retained price at last observation; all four earlier chronological price-proxy rules had negative mean gross return. Short/censored histories, selective populations, sparse sampling, non-executable prices, and unverified publisher fill records prevent those results from establishing a live edge. The supported engineering direction is better point-in-time evidence of every candidate, quote/no-quote, entry attempt, fill/failure, exit and cost, while preserving missing/unresolved outcomes. No strategy or capital authority was changed.

An independent read-only Astra review accepted the existing RuntimeRoot compatibility implementation. New schema-v2 roots hash the sorted, validated feature list with full 256-bit SHA-256, commit the schema marker and declared transaction-version support, and default that support to `all`; explicit `legacy`/`v0` values are allowed when caller evidence supports them. Pre-hash validation rejects invalid feature arrays and non-safe epoch/context values without JSON coercion. Markerless historical v1 roots retain the old ordered hash preimage, 16-hex digest, and `all` support requirement. Existing certificate readers accept both paths and frozen certificate fixtures remain v1. The focused RuntimeRoot schema suite passed 9/9 and the full suite also passed. This establishes deterministic format compatibility, not authenticated RPC runtime identity or actual feature activation.

The next concrete reliability issue was the runtime identity scan's unbounded directory collection and `Promise.all` over every file. It now streams directory entries with `fs/promises.opendir`, caps traversal at 100,000 entries and 10,000 artifacts, rejects an artifact above 32 MiB or a snapshot above 128 MiB, reads files in bounded chunks with at most four concurrent files, and maps runtime scan failures to `UNKNOWN`. The limits are above the current checkout's observed scan of 2,935 eligible files / 33,361,895 bytes. A full local scan returned `UNCHANGED_SINCE_STARTUP` with matching fingerprints. Five focused tests cover stable and changed snapshots, missing files, per-file/aggregate byte limits, and fail-closed unknown status.

Node.js v24.17 primary documentation confirms async iteration over `opendir()` automatically closes the directory handle and `FileHandle.read()` reports actual bytes read, requiring callers to handle short reads; the bounded reader loops until EOF and enforces its byte ceiling. Sources: [Node.js v24.17 filesystem API](https://nodejs.org/download/release/v24.17.0/docs/api/fs.html#class-fsdir), [FileHandle read semantics](https://nodejs.org/download/release/v24.17.0/docs/api/fs.html#filehandlereadbuffer-offset-length-position).

Verification: `node --test test/platform/runtime-root-hash-schema.test.mjs` passed 9/9; `node --test terminal/test/runtime-identity.test.mjs` passed 5/5; `npm test` exited 0 with core 396, intelligence 551, platform 847 and terminal 302 tests (2,096 total); `node --check` passed on the runtime identity module and test; `git diff --check` passed with only existing CRLF conversion notices. No terminal process was restarted, and the substantial pre-existing worktree changes were preserved.

Next: design and independently audit branch-aware capture for candidate rejection, missing/failed quotes, build/retry, partial/full exits, and unresolved settlement; then connect verified capture paths to Engine events. Keep production spool integration, single-writer enforcement, salvage/directory durability, loaded-code identity, and the paper terminal's observed source/runtime mismatch as unresolved. ## Research cycle 81 — classify candidate build failures without treating them as execution outcomes — 2026-10-06

Rechecked the live checkout, the project-chat “Solana Simulation Evidence” notes (including the existing cycle 39 transaction-version boundary), candidate Engine event flow, the retained audit reader, and the operator-side parser. Candidate `candidate_order_build_failed_v1` events already kept raw errors, but the read-only UI projection deliberately omitted those arbitrary strings and therefore could not group stale market snapshots, unsupported routes, quote validation, or other failures consistently.

Added a bounded `failureClass` taxonomy to the existing failure event. Exact known local builder errors map to `STALE_MARKET_SNAPSHOT`, `ENTRY_DISABLED_AFTER_GRADUATION`, `ROUTE_NOT_CONFIGURED`, `QUOTE_REJECTED`, `ROUTE_ASSEMBLY_REJECTED`, or `LOCAL_QUOTE_INPUT_INVALID`; all other failures remain `UNCLASSIFIED_BUILD_FAILURE`. The server reader and UI parser accept only those values. Raw diagnostic text remains out of the UI projection. The end-to-end settlement test now drives both a stale-snapshot failure and an unrelated provider failure, verifies both remain in retained attempt events with distinct identities, then confirms the following paper attempt can proceed. This enriches local build evidence only; it makes no claim about RPC acceptance, transaction landing, chain settlement, or realized profitability.

Fresh official Solana documentation confirms the v1 transaction format is active and that reading it is an opt-in compatibility change. It states that web3.js v1 reads v1 from 1.99.0 onward but cannot build or send v1 transactions; this checkout pins 1.98.4, whose actual `TransactionVersionStruct` accepts only `legacy` and `0`. The current direct exact-byte signing path therefore remains explicitly legacy/v0 only, as recorded in the earlier Simulation Evidence audit. `Executor.reconcile()` previously requested `maxSupportedTransactionVersion: 1` despite that decoder limit. It now requests `0`; any v1-only endpoint rejection remains pending because `Promise.allSettled` cannot establish all-endpoint expiry from a failed lookup. It cannot prove no-land or successful settlement. Keep v1 as a visibility/compatibility gap and do not broaden the schema-v2 `all` support field or claim v1 execution support without an end-to-end reviewed reader, builder, simulation, resource-config, signer, and reconciliation path. Sources: [Solana versioned transactions](https://solana.com/docs/core/transactions/versioned-transactions), [Solana `sendTransaction`](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/http/sendtransaction), [RPC JSON structures](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/json-structures).

Verification on the current changes: engine TypeScript build passed; terminal production bundle build passed; core plus settlement tests passed 39/39, including the pinned-SDK version request and unresolved-v1 behavior; engine audit projection and UI parser suites passed 20/20. The prior full-suite result (2,096 tests) predates this cycle's failure-class and reconciliation-version changes, so it is not represented as current full-suite evidence. No live paper service was restarted or modified.

Next: evaluate a maintained v1-capable reader for reconciliation only, with fixture-backed malformed/versioned response coverage, while preserving UNKNOWN/pending semantics; do not broaden the signer or transaction authority as a side effect. In parallel, branch-aware durable capture still needs a canonical integration for filter outcomes, no-quote, retries, partial exits, and unresolved settlement; the research spool remains manually drained and absent from production Engine composition.

## Research cycle 82 — reject an incomplete v1 reconciliation opt-in — 2026-10-06

Checked current official Solana transaction/version docs and the Anza-maintained Kit SDK against this checkout's pinned `@solana/web3.js` 1.98.4. Solana documents v1 as active, with opt-in RPC reads, inline accounts, a 4,096-byte wire limit, and resource limits embedded in the message; it also documents `@solana/kit` as supporting v1 reads. This is not a safe one-line change to `maxSupportedTransactionVersion`: the existing call uses `Connection.getTransaction`, whose pinned SDK response decoder itself only supports legacy/v0. Raising the request option to 1 would still leave this code on a decoder that cannot represent the returned v1 transaction. The transaction compatibility policy module's V1 enum is not evidence of real RPC decoding.

Kept the fail-closed reconciliation request at version 0. On unsupported-version errors, `Promise.allSettled` still prevents an erroneous expired verdict and the result stays pending. The exact-byte signing/artifact reader remains legacy/v0 only, consistent with the prior Solana Simulation Evidence boundary. No dependency or lockfile was changed, and no support is claimed for v1 settlement visibility.

A proper reader-only integration must demonstrate the entire raw JSON-RPC path: preserve existing endpoint selection, timeout/rate-limit/failover telemetry and finalized commitment; request version 1; validate response envelope, version and signatures; consume only complete `accountKeys`, pre/post lamport balances, token balances and execution status; distinguish missing metadata, provider disagreement and malformed results as unresolved; and prove that only an independently verified finalized successful observation can produce fill deltas. It must remain isolated from construction, simulation, signing and sending. The current ordinary web3.js `Connection` path does not meet these conditions, so the upgrade is deferred on evidence, not treated as implemented.

Fresh sources: [Solana versioned transactions](https://solana.com/docs/core/transactions/versioned-transactions), [Solana RPC JSON structures](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/json-structures), [Anza Kit SDK](https://github.com/anza-xyz/kit). No source code changes were retained in this cycle; the exploratory request-option and fixture edits were reverted after confirming that they would not enable decoding.

Next: if v1 reconciliation becomes a priority, add a narrow raw-RPC reader owned by the existing pool (or upgrade the client only after compatibility review), then fixture-test real v1, v0/legacy, null, failed, malformed, partial, and disagreeing endpoint responses. Continue separately on production research-spool integration and branch-aware capture of rejected/no-quote/build/retry/partial-exit/unresolved-settlement evidence.

## Research cycle 83 — connect the durable research spool to the owned Engine lifecycle — 2026-10-06

Re-read the prior spool audits (cycles 68–77), current production `runEngine()` composition, Store atomic event-ID/journal persistence contracts, shutdown flow, and the production wallet lock. The production launcher binds an exclusive loopback port derived from the wallet before creating the Store; a second production Engine for that wallet cannot reach spool construction. The spool is now constructed adjacent to the selected database only after that ownership guard is held, and its recovered records are replayed before the dashboard is exposed. Engine also performs bounded startup, periodic (five-second cadence), and shutdown drains. Replay errors are logged with phase, count, failed event identity and bounded error; they do not enter trade decisions, and the UI continues to treat completeness as unknown. `--check` remains free of persistent spool creation.

Reviewing the replay transaction exposed a second correctness defect: the spool advanced its in-memory queue and drained counters before confirming that the replacement journal had been written. If compaction failed, the on-disk file could still contain records that the process reported as drained. Replay now stages the remaining/drained sets, commits the compacted journal first, and only then updates the in-memory queue, dedupe indexes, and drained counters. On failure it preserves the previous pending queue; records already accepted by SQLite may be retried safely under the same stable IDs. Added fault injection proves both the in-memory and reopened-disk queues remain intact after compaction failure.

Official Node v24.17 docs were checked against the implementation: file sync/flush provides the file-content durability boundary used by the append and temporary compaction file, while the app's exclusive `server.listen` wallet ownership guard is process-scoped and released with the server handle. Sources: [Node.js file-system API](https://nodejs.org/download/release/v24.17.0/docs/api/fs.html#class-fsfilehandle), [Node.js net server listen](https://nodejs.org/download/release/v24.17.0/docs/api/net.html#serverlistenoptions-callback). The guard is specific to production `runEngine()`; direct library users that instantiate multiple spool owners for one path remain outside that contract.

Engine lifecycle tests cover startup, periodic and shutdown drains; the compaction failure test verifies retained queue state and safe retry. Focused spool tests pass 14/14. The final repository-wide `npm test` exited 0, including the engine build and all configured core, intelligence, platform and terminal suites (core 397 and terminal 302 reported; the full run reported no failing suite). `git diff --check` passed. No UI completeness state, trading authority, or transaction support was promoted.

This makes the spool lifecycle production-connected, but it is not yet a claim of lossless capture or complete candidate denominators. Directory-entry durability across sudden power loss is not guaranteed here; corrupt/torn files fail startup for manual recovery; loss-marker persistence itself can lag; operator salvage tooling is absent; and only observations already emitted by current Engine branches are captured. The 5-second replay cadence and 100-record replay default are bounded best-effort recovery, not durability proof. Next: verify/fix parent-directory durability on supported Windows filesystems or explicitly label the weaker guarantee, add a non-destructive operator salvage/export path for corrupt journals, make loss markers durable across restart, and map candidate/quote/no-route/entry/partial-exit/finalized-outcome branches into the event denominator before asserting research completeness. Preserve pending/unknown on any gap. `git diff --check` passed after the cycle changes.

## Research cycle 84 — retry pending research-loss markers through the serialized Store — 2026-10-06

Rechecked the prior durability notes against `Engine.saveState()`, worker-backed `Store.save()`, Engine tick ordering, and production recovery. A failed audit append and failed spool enqueue update the loss marker in memory, but earlier persistence relied on some later fill, position checkpoint, or shutdown path. Quiet paper runs could therefore hold a valid pending marker longer than needed. The main Engine loop now retries a pending marker at most once per second using its normal serialized Store path; it does not write from the persistence error callback or overlap a separate state writer. If that attempt fails, it logs a bounded error and leaves the status `PENDING_STATE_SAVE`; it retries on a later tick. Shutdown continues to attempt the final state save.

The production Store setup was inspected directly: `openGenerationDatabase()` selects and verifies WAL mode, `synchronous=FULL`, foreign keys, and bounded busy timeout. The state worker wraps saves in `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`/`COMMIT`. SQLite documents that WAL plus `synchronous=FULL` syncs the WAL for each commit, supporting recovery after a successful commit. This does not make a failed storage path durable: if both the original research sinks and Store are unavailable, the in-memory marker cannot guarantee survival of abrupt process or machine loss. Sources: [SQLite WAL](https://www.sqlite.org/wal.html), [SQLite synchronous pragma](https://www.sqlite.org/pragma.html#pragma_synchronous).

A new Engine regression injects an audit failure followed by a transient state-save failure, then proves the loop retries the marker without another trade and reports `PERSISTED` after the next successful commit. Focused spool/lifecycle tests pass 15/15. The final repository-wide `npm test` exited 0 with engine build and all configured suites passing (core 397 and terminal 302 reported; no suite failures). `git diff --check` passed. No active service was restarted, and no production completeness claim was added.

The marker retry deliberately calls `Store.save()` without a state-audit label so the marker commit is not rolled back by a separate audit-table insert failure. This makes it independently retryable as a state write, but not independently persisted from the database itself.

Remaining: eventual state-save retry cannot preserve the marker when the Store is permanently unavailable, and it does not provide a separate durable incident channel. Continue by giving capture-loss status an independently queryable append-only persistence path or a clearly bounded sidecar/recovery protocol, testing abrupt termination at every write boundary, and exposing the pending/invalid state without equating it with complete capture. Then expand the actual candidate denominator to include no-quote and filter-rejection paths.

## Research cycle 85 — retain rejected create observations and tracking termination — 2026-10-06

The October 5 token-data report's central limitation is selection: recorded peaks cannot be treated as representative outcomes when short, missing, or filtered histories disappear. Re-traced the actual Engine candidate path and found raw create events with invalid, implausibly future, or already-stale timestamps returned before `candidate_discovered_v1`; max-age expiry and capacity eviction also removed tracked candidates without a retained reason. This understated the Engine-observed denominator and hid when observation stopped.

The Engine now writes discovery for each create event with a bounded `discoveryDisposition` (`TRACKED` or `REJECTED_AT_DISCOVERY`) and nullable parsed chain time before deciding whether it enters evaluation. Invalid, future (>10 seconds past local receipt), and stale-at-discovery (> configured maximum age) timestamps get a distinct `candidate_tracking_ended_v1` with a reason and `outcomeStatus: UNRESOLVED`. Capacity eviction and max-age expiry emit the same event, with a point-in-time snapshot where available; capacity eviction then removes its counterfactual scan state. These events indicate tracking scope, not a trade, chain result, or resolved token performance.

The read-only Engine audit projection and browser parser now allowlist this event and reject unknown end reasons, missing generation identity, non-integer end times, or any outcome status other than `UNRESOLVED`. Older v1 discovery payloads remain projectable because the new disposition is not a required field. Existing discovery identities and build/attempt IDs are preserved. Pump's official IDL defines the `CreateEvent.timestamp` field as signed `i64`; this supports validation of the source field's intended type, while the local event decoder remains responsible for its runtime representation: [official Pump program IDL](https://github.com/pump-fun/pump-public-docs/blob/main/idl/pump.json).

Regression coverage exercises malformed timestamp discovery, unresolved capacity eviction, reader identity projection, and UI parsing. `npm run build:engine`, terminal production bundle build, focused tests (22/22), the full `npm test` (core 397, intelligence 551, platform 850, terminal 302), and `git diff --check` passed.

This only closes omissions after the Engine receives a create event. It cannot count launches missed by the provider, subscription, decoder, retention window, or prior process outage. A source-coverage certificate and non-destructive historical backfill are still absent. Next highest-value work: quantify capture coverage against independent slot/signature or account-history evidence, then record no-quote and pre-build filter branches with consistent candidate-generation identity. Keep all unobserved or censored outcomes UNKNOWN/UNRESOLVED.

## Research cycle 86 — keep filtered transaction receipts out of the continuous slot frontier — 2026-10-06

Revalidated current GitHub `main` with `git ls-remote`: it still resolves to `373143a10d05327149cc5d3cbd0cfb44bff9cc18`, matching the local `main`/`origin/main` base beneath this checkout's pre-existing dirty work. GitHub currently shows zero issues and zero pull requests. Revisited the token-data audit and earlier Solana Simulation Evidence conclusions: denominator and completeness claims require a source-specific coverage measure; transaction observations alone cannot be relabeled as block coverage.

The current Feed subscribes to `logsSubscribe` with a Pump-program `mentions` filter and separately consumes a filtered Yellowstone transaction stream. Solana defines `mentions` as returning only matching transactions. Feed passed each accepted transaction slot to `IngestionGapReconciler.registerSlot(..., false)`, correctly suppressing missing-slot alarms but still moving its single `latestContinuousSlot` to the observed slot. That let a non-contiguous filtered observation silently become the base of a field named as continuous coverage. The class has no lane-specific frontier, and source inspection found no production backfill handler connected to Feed.

`registerSlot` now treats `expectContiguous: false` as receipt-only: it can still be retained in the bounded seen-slot ring, but it cannot move the continuous coverage frontier. The first `expectContiguous: true` observation establishes a baseline; gaps are checked from then onward, and prior history remains unknown absent separate coverage evidence. Startup self-test now executes the filtered-receipt, baseline, and gap-detection cases instead of merely checking that a method exists. Regression coverage confirms filtered observations leave the frontier at zero, a later complete-source observation establishes the baseline, and the next discontinuity is detected.

Protocol research rules out promoting the existing WebSocket filter as a census. Solana's `blockSubscribe` can stream blocks, but the method is explicitly unstable and requires validator-side flags. The documented RPC alternative is bounded `getBlocks` over confirmed ranges followed by `getBlock` to retrieve block transactions; this could support a separate historical coverage adapter, but has provider history, transaction-version, response-size, and exact-fork verification requirements. Sources: [Solana `logsSubscribe`](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/websocket/logssubscribe), [Solana `blockSubscribe`](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/websocket/blocksubscribe), [Solana `getBlocks`](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/http/getblocks), [Solana RPC method index (`getBlock`)](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/http).

The computer-use helper could not initialize in this environment (kernel-assets path error on both attempts); GitHub HEAD/issues/PRs and protocol sources were checked through direct read-only GitHub and Solana documentation pages instead.
