## Research cycle 87 — require range-bound, bounded recovery evidence — 2026-10-06

Continued from the Solana token-data research and the “Solana Simulation Evidence” history, including the transaction-version visibility boundary and the prior cycle’s finding that filtered Pump transaction streams cannot certify chain-wide slot coverage. The reconciler’s backfill interface still accepted a plain boolean `true`, and its public resolution method could mark a gap complete without a range certificate. Large gaps were intentionally represented as compact intervals, so treating interval completion as verified per-slot recovery would also overstate coverage.

Changed the handler contract to return a `RecoveryCertificate` only. The reconciler now requires the certificate to bind exactly to the outstanding gap’s ID, range, provider, classification, and lane; carry full 256-bit lowercase state and coverage roots; have a valid certification time; and account for every covered slot with a non-`UNAVAILABLE` status. Certificates that exceed the configured bounded range, omit or add slot statuses, reuse an accepted certificate ID, contain accessors/unknown fields, or use malformed arrays and objects are rejected. Accepted certificate data is copied into frozen snapshots before storage, preventing later caller mutation from changing the evidence. Large gaps remain compact and unresolved until an explicit bounded proof strategy exists. A directly received late slot may shrink an interval only when `expectContiguous` is true and provider, classification, and lane match; such a receipt is not counted as historical backfill. Filtered and foreign-provider receipts cannot shrink coverage gaps, and an earlier filtered receipt no longer suppresses a later contiguous receipt for that same slot.

This validator checks structural consistency and caller attestation; it does not authenticate the provider, recompute roots, independently fetch blocks, or prove canonical/finalized chain coverage. `isVerified` remains a trust assertion at this boundary. A production recovery adapter must still be designed and independently reviewed, with provider provenance, blockhash/fork and commitment checks, transaction-version handling, pruning/partial-response states, and independent comparison against observed Pump transactions. No live execution or capital authority consumes these certificates.

Fresh primary RPC documentation sharpens the adapter contract: `getBlocks` returns confirmed block slots in an inclusive range and omits skipped slots; its maximum range is 500,000 slots. Thus the adapter must validate the returned slot list against the requested interval and may classify omitted slots as skipped only under the documented method semantics and an explicit provider response, while a failed/truncated response remains unavailable. Confirmed/finalized commitment and provider ledger retention remain separate evidence. Sources: [Solana `getBlocks`](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/http/getblocks), [removed `getConfirmedBlocks` migration note](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/deprecated/getconfirmedblocks), [Solana RPC commitment levels](https://solana.com/docs/rpc).

When a matching late receipt splits a gap while an older backfill request is pending or in flight, the reconciler now replaces queued work with the exact remaining intervals. An in-flight certificate for the stale original range is rejected; child ranges are retried serially under the existing queue bound, and queue overflow marks history incomplete.

Accepted recovery certificates are also retained under a capacity-derived unit bound (each slot status and recovered event ID consumes one unit, with a fixed per-certificate unit). New verified intervals can continue to resolve by evicting the oldest retained proof, but eviction sets the existing history-truncation signal and therefore keeps `hasUnresolvedGaps()` fail-closed. Recent proofs remain queryable; the in-memory component does not claim durable archival of older certificates.

Verification: `npm run build:engine` passed; focused reconciler and PASS-24 recovery tests passed 16/16 after the in-flight split fix; after adding the retention bound, the focused reconciler suite passed 12/12. Final `npm test` exited 0 with core 397, intelligence 551, platform 855, and terminal 302 tests (2,105 total). Scoped `git diff --check` passed. The local checkout remains on the verified GitHub `main` base SHA `373143a10d05327149cc5d3cbd0cfb44bff9cc18`, with pre-existing dirty/untracked work preserved.

Next: have an independent compatibility review inspect the new certificate schema and all resolution callers, then design a bounded finalized-block audit adapter that can produce certificates from independently verifiable range evidence. Keep provider authenticity, durable lane-specific frontiers, canonical fork identity, and source completeness explicitly unresolved until that adapter exists and is verified.

## Research cycle 88 — persist verified recovery certificates before resolving gaps — 2026-10-06

Revalidated local `main` and GitHub `main`; both remain at `373143a10d05327149cc5d3cbd0cfb44bff9cc18`, with zero open issues and pull requests. Re-read the referenced Ω Capturable Multiplier blueprint, current recovery changes, and related SYLPH project chats. The blueprint requires provenance and continuity through canonical truth while treating absent proof as unknown. The latest “Solana Simulation Evidence” cycles also retain the transaction-version boundary and the absence of a production backfill handler.

The architecture check found that the existing SQLite `recovery_certificates` API predates the reconciler’s current certificate shape: it expects `fromSlot`/`toSlot` and a non-null signature, while the current type has `startSlot`/`endSlot`, explicit per-slot status, and no cryptographic signature. There were no production call sites joining these APIs. The old `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` path also allowed the same certificate ID to replace its contents.

Added a separate current-format SQLite journal and shared certificate snapshot/serialization validation. The `verified_recovery_certificates_v2` table is STRICT, checks indexed identity/range/lane/hash fields, and stores a bounded certificate JSON plus SHA-256 of its canonical snapshot. Writes are transactionally idempotent for identical content and reject a reused ID with different content. Reads verify the JSON hash and every indexed field before returning the proof. The legacy signed-row method remains for its existing callers but no longer overwrites conflicting certificate IDs or accepts the current unsigned range-certificate shape.

Connected `Engine` → `Feed` → `IngestionGapReconciler` to `Store` through the typed recovery-journal interface. Backfill processing validates the certificate against the live gap, awaits the durable write, and only then marks the gap resolved. Journal failure leaves the gap unresolved; provider replacement during a write cannot authorize the superseded result. End-to-end tests use two independent Store workers to verify insertion, retrieval by certificate/gap ID, idempotent duplicate writes, conflicting-content rejection, and journal-before-resolution ordering.

Primary SQLite research supports the concurrency and storage choices: `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` acquires the write transaction before checking/inserting, serializing competing writers, while STRICT tables and CHECK constraints enforce stored types/ranges; application-level hashing and row-to-JSON comparison remain necessary for content integrity. Sources: [SQLite transactions](https://www.sqlite.org/lang_transaction.html), [SQLite STRICT tables](https://www.sqlite.org/stricttables.html).

This is durable certificate plumbing, not a production recovery source. The repository still has no production `BackfillHandler`; a certificate’s `isVerified` remains a caller attestation and the hash detects accidental/content mismatch rather than authenticating a provider. Restart restoration of persisted certificates/frontiers and an independently verified finalized-block adapter remain outstanding. SQLite API conflict protection also does not make the underlying file tamper-proof against direct database edits; reads detect modified row/index/JSON state but cannot prove that an entire row was never deleted.

Final `npm test` exited 0: core 397, intelligence 551, platform 859, terminal 302 (2,109 total, zero failures). Scoped `git diff --check` and trailing-whitespace checks on new files passed. No live service was restarted or modified. Working-tree changes from earlier cycles were preserved.

The listener is Node PID 20520 running `terminal/server.mjs`, but its working directory is not established by the available process metadata. `/api/live/readiness` reports `liveReady:false`, `liveSigningUnavailable:true`, and `productionCapitalAuthorityBlocked:true` (8/10 gates pass). No service was restarted or changed.

## Research cycle 89 — compare bounded finalized block ranges without manufacturing recovery proof — 2026-10-06

Read the supplied October 5 token-outcomes report and the saved project research around “Solana Simulation Evidence,” “Upgrades,” “Sol,” and “SOL_SYLPH.” The token report's censored launch histories and negative price-proxy exit tests support better point-in-time evidence and honest unresolved outcomes; they do not establish a buy/exit edge. No trading policy, live execution authority, or capital setting changed. The RuntimeRoot v2 compatibility requirement was already implemented and independently reviewed in prior work: new roots carry a strict full 256-bit feature hash, while markerless v1 keeps its old 16-hex digest and default support value. That change was not repeated.

The cycle-88 recovery journal is now durable, but still has no production source capable of independently creating valid range evidence. Added `auditFinalizedBlockRange` and connected it read-only to `RpcPool`. The adapter caps ranges at 256 slots by defaulting to 64, restricts endpoint count and global block-fetch concurrency, verifies each endpoint reports mainnet genesis, reads its finalized watermark and earliest retained block, requests only the intersection of finalized/retained history, and rechecks retention before interpreting omitted slots. Thus slots above an endpoint's finalized watermark and slots outside its retained range cannot be mislabeled as skipped. Within stable reported bounds, omitted slots are classified as skipped according to Solana's documented `getBlocks` behavior. Each listed block is fetched finalized with full details and `maxSupportedTransactionVersion: 0`; unsupported v1 responses, null blocks, malformed data, null metadata, provider disagreement, and inconsistent adjacent parent links remain explicit states.

The result includes bounded per-provider and per-slot observations, point-in-time audit timestamps, comparison fingerprints, and limitations. Fingerprints compare the endpoint-reported blockhash, parent linkage, transaction versions, and ordered signatures; they do not rederive blockhashes, prove provider independence, authenticate RPC claims, or verify transaction effects. Most importantly, this adapter returns `authorityEligible: false`, emits no `RecoveryCertificate`, does not resolve a gap, and cannot advance a coverage frontier. Connecting it to any recovery authority remains blocked on independent review of provider/fork semantics, authenticated provenance, durable restoration, and the certificate contract.

This matches the current Solana references: `getBlocks` takes an inclusive range and commitment, its predecessor migration note says skipped slots remain omitted, `getFirstAvailableBlock` reports the lowest confirmed block retained by the node, and `getBlock` may return null and requires an explicit maximum transaction-version setting. The official versioned-transaction guide documents that a v1 transaction can make a whole `getBlock` request fail when the client specifies version 0; the code therefore reports this as unsupported rather than treating it as an empty block. Sources: [Solana getBlocks](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/http/getblocks), [skipped-slot migration note](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/deprecated/getconfirmedblocks), [Solana getBlock](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/http/getblock), [Solana RPC methods and getFirstAvailableBlock](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/http), [versioned transactions](https://solana.com/docs/core/transactions/versioned-transactions).

## Research cycle 92 — require range bounds to cover the audit on both sides of reads — 2026-10-06

Continued the same source-driven Solana recovery audit against the current working tree and pinned web3.js 1.98.4. Official Solana RPC documentation says `getBlocks` returns block slots in an inclusive range, the Agave 2.0 migration note explicitly says skipped slots remain omitted, `getBlock` returns a block or null, and `getFirstAvailableBlock` reports the lowest confirmed block still in that node's ledger. Those references establish documented API semantics, not provider truth or archive completeness. Sources: [getBlocks](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/http/getblocks), [getBlock](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/http/getblock), [getFirstAvailableBlock](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/http/getfirstavailableblock), [removed getConfirmedBlocks migration note](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/deprecated/getconfirmedblocks).

Code review found that the auditor rechecked history retention after reads but did not refresh the endpoint's finalized watermark before interpreting `getBlocks` omissions. Added a final parallel read of finalized slot and first available block. A slot omitted by `getBlocks` is now called skipped only if refreshed bounds are valid and still contain that slot; moved-behind-finality omissions become `NOT_FINALIZED_AT_ENDPOINT`, newly pruned omissions become `HISTORY_PRUNED_OR_UNAVAILABLE`, and an unavailable/invalid refresh makes omissions `RANGE_UNAVAILABLE`. Successfully fetched blocks remain direct endpoint observations when the refresh is unavailable; if refreshed valid bounds put a fetched slot outside their range, that response is held as `RANGE_UNAVAILABLE`. New tests cover finality regression, retention movement, failed refresh, and preservation of fetched-block evidence.

## Research cycle 94 — map the recovery-verifier trust boundary and evidence the next design — 2026-10-06

Revalidated the current worktree (`main` at `373143a10d05327149cc5d3cbd0cfb44bff9cc18` with prior local edits still present), remote `main` (same SHA), and the public repository pages (zero open issues and zero pull requests). Focused and full-suite coverage is recorded in cycle 93; this extra assertion is to be rerun with the final check below.

External design research: the current TUF specification requires threshold signatures, trusted-root chaining across rotations, monotonically increasing root versions to prevent rollback, and metadata expiration to bound freeze attacks. Node 24.21's official crypto documentation includes signature verification APIs; the repository targets Node 24, so a standard-library verifier is technically available. Applying those software-update controls to capital recovery is an engineering inference, not something TUF specifies for runtime trading authority. The key finding is that choosing Ed25519 is insufficient by itself: SYLPH still needs a provisioned trust root, issuer/role scope, canonical signed bytes, threshold policy, revocation/rotation, anti-rollback state, expiry/freshness checks, and audited evidence references. No trustworthy issuer/key configuration exists in the repository, so the safe implementation remains fail-closed. Sources: [TUF specification v1.0.36](https://theupdateframework.github.io/specification/v1.0.36/), [Node.js v24.21 crypto documentation](https://nodejs.org/download/release/v24.21.0/docs/api/crypto.html).

## Research cycle 97 — prevent BuildSeal caller claims from granting runtime signing authority — 2026-10-06

Continued the release-trust audit from cycle 96, including prior “Upgrades,” “Sol,” “SOL_SYLPH,” and “Solana Simulation Evidence” research and the current release/readiness notes. The BuildSeal source has no production call sites, but its exported authority API and its test claimed runtime signing was allowed when a caller set `isAuthorized: true`. Alternatively, `registerReleaseCertificate` accepted a `PRODUCTION_CERTIFIED` label and any non-empty signature string without checking a signature, issuer identity, or trust root. A self-asserted certificate/root could therefore pass the class's in-memory gate if bytecode and Node version matched. The repository has no provisioned release trust root or trusted verifier wired to this class.

Changed BuildSeal to compute candidate release identity using the repository's canonical SHA-256 JSON hasher, with an explicit schema label and no caller authorization bit in the measured build identity. Inputs for the artifact map and optional certificate are snapshotted/frozen in the returned root. Candidate roots are always `isAuthorized: false`; certificate registration always rejects because there is no verifier; and runtime signing remains denied even when node/artifact observations match. Node mismatch and artifact tampering continue to produce specific diagnostics. Copied/modified root objects do not pass the registered-candidate identity check. This is a contained block, not completed release certification; no software can safely make this path authorize signing until a trust root and verifier lifecycle are configured and independently reviewed.

External research: Node v24.21 documents Ed25519 signature verification APIs, so cryptographic verification primitives exist in the project's supported runtime. That primitive alone does not establish trust. TUF's release-metadata design provides relevant security controls by requiring a shipped trust root, threshold signatures, chained root rotation, monotonically increasing versions, and metadata expiry. Applying those software-update controls to BuildSeal is an engineering inference; TUF does not directly specify trading-runtime authority. Sources: [Node.js v24.21 crypto documentation](https://nodejs.org/download/release/v24.21.0/docs/api/crypto.html), [TUF specification v1.0.36](https://theupdateframework.github.io/specification/v1.0.36/), [repository README](https://github.com/MrPineapples22/sylph-fusion#readme).

## Research cycle 98 — pin issuer identity to public keys and remove ephemeral authority keys — 2026-10-06

Continued the BuildSeal trust review against the issuer-manifest implementation, its Section 64 caller, and signing/release documentation. `AuthorityIssuerRegistry` had generated 13 Ed25519 keypairs on construction, so a default registry silently created signing keys for every role in process memory. Verification then looked up a role but did not bind the envelope's issuer ID or key ID to that role, and passed private-key material to `verify`. The only repository call site was a test, so no production runtime depended on the implicit generated identities.

Replaced default key generation with an empty-by-default registry that accepts an explicit role identity, a pinned Ed25519 public key, and an optional external synchronous signer capability. The configured public-key fingerprint must match its DER SubjectPublicKeyInfo bytes; private KeyObjects and non-public PEM inputs are rejected. One role cannot reuse another role's issuer ID, key ID, or public-key fingerprint. Signing validates exact SHA-256 digest format and checks returned signatures against the pinned public key before returning an envelope. Verification snapshots exact data-only envelope fields, rejects proxies/accessors/extra fields and malformed hex, and binds role, issuer ID, key ID, algorithm, digest and signature to the configured public key. The test now creates its ephemeral keypair itself, outside production code, and covers mismatch, replayed identity, proxy, extra-field, cross-role key reuse and bad signer results.

This is an issuer verifier primitive, not a release trust lifecycle. There is still no operator-provisioned issuer manifest, durable key rotation/revocation or anti-rollback state, threshold policy, expiry, or runtime integration. A configured callback is an external signing capability; the code verifies its result but cannot prove the custody or governance of that signer. No production key configuration was invented.

The design follows the documented Node v24 Ed25519 `sign`/`verify` APIs and the same trust lesson from TUF: a valid signature only means a trusted key signed bytes; the system must first establish which key is trusted and how trust rotates and expires. The TUF analogy is for release metadata, not a direct requirement for SYLPH runtime signatures. Sources: [Node.js v24.21 crypto documentation](https://nodejs.org/download/release/v24.21.0/docs/api/crypto.html), [TUF specification v1.0.36](https://theupdateframework.github.io/specification/v1.0.36/).

Public GitHub `main`, local `main`, and `origin/main` remain at `373143a10d05327149cc5d3cbd0cfb44bff9cc18`; current public pages show zero open issues and pull requests. The working tree remains uncommitted and retains all previous user changes; no service was restarted.

Next: inspect the other exported certificate and issuer verifiers for the same identity-binding, key-provisioning and lifecycle assumptions, especially the currently unconnected release certificate verification path. Keep production signing blocked until a real trust root, durable rotation/revocation contract and runtime-bound release evidence are provisioned and independently reviewed.

## Research cycle 99 — require signature verification before an ActionProofBundle can authorize — 2026-10-06

Continued the issuer trust audit through `ProofArtifact`, `ActionProofBundle`, and `UnifiedPipelineUnit.compileAndAuthorizeProof`. The bundle validator checked certificate presence, labels, roles, time/slot bounds, roots, and epochs, but never verified the artifact signatures. It could therefore return `isAuthorized: true` from structurally plausible caller-built certificate objects. The unified pipeline generated HMAC artifacts from one caller-provided signing string with role-name suffixes, then passed the validator without a verifier capability; its test described the resulting bundle as cryptographically authorized even though the kernel remained `A0_OBSERVE_ONLY`. There are no other source callers of this validation method.

Added an explicit `TrustedProofArtifactVerifier` capability to bundle validation. Every required artifact now must be accepted by this verifier; missing verifier state or verifier errors make the bundle unauthorized, and invalid signatures are reported as blockers. Structural, role, root, time, slot, and epoch checks remain in place. The UnifiedPipelineUnit has no trusted artifact verifier configured, so Stage 3 now correctly stops with `PROOF_BUNDLE_INVALID` instead of presenting caller-generated HMACs as independent authority. Tests use an explicitly named local test-key verifier to exercise the successful validator path and assert that the same well-shaped bundle remains blocked without that capability; tampered signatures fail.

This does not configure the verifier for production. The remaining HMAC ProofArtifact format accepts a caller-supplied shared secret and does not independently establish issuer key custody or role-specific trust. A runtime verifier must be provided by trusted composition and should validate a public-key signature against an operator-provisioned issuer manifest, bound to the exact canonical artifact payload and issuer role. The release and live signer paths remain blocked until this is done.

Fresh primary-source research supports this boundary: NIST SP 800-57 states that a self-signed certificate protects integrity but does not by itself guarantee authenticity; authenticity depends on secure distribution of trust material. Node v24.21 provides the Ed25519 verification primitive, but the project still needs to distribute and manage trusted public keys. Applying the NIST key-management framing to this application is an engineering interpretation. Sources: [NIST SP 800-57 Part 1 Rev. 5](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/specialpublications/nist.sp.800-57pt1r5.pdf), [Node.js v24.21 crypto documentation](https://nodejs.org/download/release/v24.21.0/docs/api/crypto.html).

Public repository state was refreshed this turn and remains at main SHA `373143a10d05327149cc5d3cbd0cfb44bff9cc18`, with zero open issues and pull requests. Scoped whitespace validation passed; the working tree remains uncommitted with all prior changes retained, and no service was restarted.

Next: replace research-only HMAC proof production with an explicit issuer-verification format only after a trusted issuer manifest and external key custody are defined. Audit the remaining `isAuthorized` and `verify*Certificate` call sites for structural checks that are presented as cryptographic proof, and keep missing verifier capabilities fail-closed.

## Research cycle 100 — harden ProofArtifact hashing and reject executable input shapes — 2026-10-06

Continued the exported proof-artifact review from cycle 99. The artifact verifier was still using delimiter-style content hashing, accepted object properties through ordinary JSON serialization (which can invoke getters or proxy traps), allowed unbounded/cyclic structures to reach hashing, and compared HMAC bytes with a regular equality path. Replaced those paths with bounded data-only JSON snapshots that reject proxies, getters/setters, sparse arrays, symbols, cycles, unsupported prototypes, non-finite numbers, excessive depth, node counts, and encoded size. The digest now uses the shared canonical SHA-256 hasher, validates the exact envelope field set, and compares fixed-length signatures with Node's timing-safe comparison. Verification explicitly projects signed fields before hashing so envelope-only fields do not contaminate digest construction. A follow-up review found the shared canonical serializer treated an own `__proto__` property as a prototype setter, which could omit signed data from hashes. Changed its normalized object to a null-prototype map so that property remains ordinary JSON data; regression tests prove deterministic ordering and distinct digests. The proof snapshot also measures the complete canonical UTF-8 encoding against the 1 MiB ceiling. HMAC remains an integrity check for a verifier-held secret and does not establish issuer identity; the ActionProofBundle capability gate from cycle 99 continues to block authorization until a trusted verifier is configured.

Added regressions proving accessors are not invoked, proxies are rejected without traps, nested key order canonicalizes, and delimiter-shaped values remain distinct. The test also preserves the existing valid artifact, tampered payload, invalid signature, revocation, bundle and end-to-end pipeline behavior.

External research against primary standards and runtime documentation: Node v24.21's official crypto documentation says `timingSafeEqual` compares bytes with a constant-time algorithm, but cautions that surrounding code is not thereby timing-safe; the project uses it only for equal-length validated digests. RFC 8785 describes invariant canonical representations for cryptographic operations and constrains input to I-JSON. The project serializer has additional semantics for bigint, Date, Set and Map, so it is not claimed to be JCS; the proof snapshot constrains the input to bounded JSON values, and cross-language conformance remains an open verification need. NIST SP 800-57 Part 1 Rev. 5 frames key authenticity around secure trust-material distribution. The implication for this code is limited: the local HMAC format can protect integrity when both sides already trust the same secret, but it cannot identify an issuer or support production authority by itself. Sources: [Node.js v24.21 crypto documentation](https://nodejs.org/download/release/v24.21.0/docs/api/crypto.html), [RFC 8785 JSON Canonicalization Scheme](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8785.html), [NIST SP 800-57 Part 1 Rev. 5](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/specialpublications/nist.sp.800-57pt1r5.pdf).

The highest-value unresolved trust dependency remains the absent operator-provisioned issuer root, key rotation/revocation and verifier composition; do not re-enable certificate-driven authority until those are defined and independently reviewed.

## Research cycle 101 — expose typed paper quote and fee evidence in the Engine audit — 2026-10-06

Compared the October 5 token-data report's requirement for size- and timing-aware entry/exit evidence with current production `Engine` event writers in `src/fusion.ts`, `Store.appendAuditEvent`, the read-only `terminal/engine-research-audit.mjs` allowlist, the React audit panel, and the legacy `EconomicFlightRecorder` store. The live Engine already writes candidate discovery, gate, build, tracking-end, and paper-fill observations into its own audit table, and the terminal reads those observations. The separate legacy flight-recorder database still has no production lifecycle writers, so it remains a separate prototype and is not used as the source for these events.

The source events placed fee details in nested `overhead` objects; the audit's deliberate scalar allowlist therefore hid those details from the terminal. Added explicit scalar fields for base fee, prioritization fee, Jito tip, rent, modeled slippage, sell-side modeled slippage amount, requested/quoted raw amounts, input/output units, and market-snapshot age. The rows are labeled `PAPER_BUILD_ESTIMATE` or `PAPER_SIMULATED_FILL`; their output units distinguish buy token raw units from sell SOL lamports, and their inputs distinguish buy SOL lamports from sell token raw units. The UI prints exact integer strings and never infers decimal-adjusted token quantities. Sell-only slippage fields are omitted on buys. The parser rejects malformed integer strings, invalid units, side/unit mismatches, inconsistent evidence labels, and malformed age/bps values, while unknown nested data and errors remain omitted. This extends the existing audit table and its read-only projection rather than creating a dual-write recorder path.

External primary-source review informed the labels: Solana documents base and prioritization fees as separate transaction-fee components; Jito documents its tip as a separate auction input for block-engine submission, so the panel keeps them distinct. Here they remain modeled paper values, and slippage basis points are not actual observed slippage. Sources: [Solana fee documentation](https://solana.com/docs/core/fees), [Solana fee structure](https://solana.com/docs/core/fees/fee-structure), [Jito low-latency transaction and bundle documentation](https://docs.jito.wtf/lowlatencytxnsend/).

## Research cycle 102 — expose bounded candidate restriction reasons — 2026-10-06

Revisited the supplied October token cohort report and the related SOL-SYLPH/Upgrades research conversations. Their recurring limitation is selection and missing outcomes: observing only candidates that progress past filters does not describe the full funnel, and a candidate that stops being tracked is not a resolved loss or win. The Engine already records candidate restrictions, gate failures and classified build failures, but the read-only audit projection omitted restriction scope, effect and reason. As a result, a retained row could show that a candidate was restricted without showing why.

Added a finite `reasonCode` at the restriction event writer and project the code with validated scope/effect fields. Codes come from a fixed internal set; dynamic capital-barrier text and candidate evaluation exceptions collapse to stable categories. The projection explicitly removes legacy free-form restriction `reason` text, while preserving the existing `reason` field for tracking-ended records. The UI now shows restriction, submission-block, and classified build-failure context. The browser parser validates these enum fields and rejects raw restriction reason text. New fields remain optional when reading prior v1 restriction records, preserving compatibility with older retained rows. No candidate decision or execution behavior changed.

Fresh external guidance supports the shape of this narrow projection: OpenTelemetry event conventions recommend occurrence-specific attributes useful for filtering/grouping and distinguish named events from dynamic attribute values; OWASP recommends recording outcome/reason context while excluding secrets and validating event fields. These are design references, not a claim that this Engine audit implements OpenTelemetry or a security-grade audit trail. Sources: [OpenTelemetry event conventions](https://opentelemetry.io/docs/specs/semconv/general/events/), [OWASP Logging Cheat Sheet](https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/Logging_Cheat_Sheet.html).

Remaining: the source captures only Engine-observed candidates; provider omissions, shutdown windows, and asynchronous persistence can still make the denominator incomplete. The existing production spool provides bounded best-effort recovery, but ordinary observations are not synchronously spooled before the database write is posted, so an abrupt process loss before database commit can escape the persisted loss marker. Do not claim crash-lossless capture. ## Research cycle 103 — close the candidate-execution audit crash window — 2026-10-06

Followed the cycle-102 durability question through the actual `Engine.persistResearchObservation()` → `Store.appendAuditEvent()` → SQLite worker path. A child-process reproduction posted the write and immediately called `process.exit(0)` without awaiting the worker acknowledgement; the first ten trials each reopened without the row. This is a reproducibility probe, not a production loss-rate estimate. SQLite's writer already commits each audit row and stable-ID dedupe record in one transaction, so the observed gap was before the asynchronous worker completed that commit. SQLite documents that WAL with `synchronous=FULL` syncs the WAL at each commit; the app still needs to preserve an observation until it knows that commit completed. Node documents `fsyncSync()` as a request to flush file data to storage. Sources: [SQLite synchronous pragma](https://www.sqlite.org/pragma.html#pragma_synchronous), [Node.js file system API](https://nodejs.org/api/fs.html).

Execution lifecycle audit events (gate pass, submission block, order-build start/failure/success/abandonment, and paper fill) now synchronously enter the existing bounded durable spool before the asynchronous Store write is posted. The same stable event ID is used on direct write and replay, so a crash after SQLite commit but before acknowledgement is repaired idempotently. Child-process tests force termination both immediately after the Engine's writer call (before commit) and after SQLite commit but before spool compaction. Restart restores the first record and deduplicates the second; both leave exactly one SQLite row. This synchronous work is limited to low-volume execution lifecycle events; high-rate discovery/snapshot events remain on the asynchronous path to avoid putting file fsync on feed ingestion. If spool capacity or disk write fails, evidence loss is recorded and execution behavior remains unchanged.

Verification: `npm run build:engine` passed. Focused durable-spool tests passed 17/17; the child-process cases cover both sides of SQLite's commit boundary. Full `npm test` passed immediately before the second process-death case was added: core 397, intelligence 551, platform 881, terminal 303 (2,132 total). The final platform rerun, including both crash cases, passed 882/882, and focused RuntimeRoot schema tests passed 9/9. The RuntimeRoot v2 compatibility rules mentioned in the project request were rechecked against their source/tests and prior independent acceptance record: newly created roots use a full 64-hex feature digest, reject invalid feature lists/epoch/context before hashing, default transaction support to `all`, permit explicit enum declarations, and preserve markerless v1's original ordered preimage and 16-hex digest. The saved Solana Simulation Evidence work was also reviewed; no simulation-to-signing authority or transaction-version support was inferred or added.

Remaining: this closes only the app-process gap for the named low-volume events when spool admission succeeds. Feed observations, provider omissions, spool capacity/disk failure, filesystem/hardware durability limits, and the incomplete candidate denominator remain unresolved. The current portable Node implementation fsyncs spool file contents but has no verified cross-platform parent-directory fsync after create/rename/unlink; sudden power-loss durability of directory-entry changes therefore remains filesystem/platform dependent. The spool uses synchronous file I/O on execution lifecycle paths; the earlier machine-specific p50/p95/p99/max append measurements were 0.58/0.87/1.27/1.71 ms, so event-loop cost should be monitored under real paper workloads. Do not call this audit complete, lossless, chain evidence, or a production profitability result. Next: measure spool occupancy and per-event append latency in the existing paper soak telemetry without emitting per-feed sync writes, then improve the bounded funnel's explicit unknown and pruned counts.

## Research cycle 104 — serialize spool owners by storage identity — 2026-10-06

The durable-spool contract says append rollback and compaction require one writer, and `runEngine()` constructed the spool beside `DB_PATH`. The existing process lease was keyed only by wallet. Since callers can provide a distinct explicit paper wallet while sharing a configured database path, that lease did not itself enforce the spool's ownership contract. SQLite supports multiple connections with one writer at a time, but its locking does not serialize mutation of this separate JSONL spool. Official Node documentation confirms that an exclusive `net.Server.listen()` prevents a second process from sharing the same bound port; the implementation uses this as a same-host process lease. Sources: [SQLite isolation and single-writer behavior](https://www.sqlite.org/isolation.html), [Node.js net server listen](https://nodejs.org/api/net.html#serverlisten).

Added a second process lease derived from the resolved spool path, disjoint from the existing wallet-lock port range. Existing path and parent-directory real paths are canonicalized before hashing, with case folding on Windows. The runner creates the database parent directory, acquires the spool lease, and only then opens the Store or constructs the spool; it holds the lease through shutdown and releases it in `finally`. A regression test binds the lease, confirms both an in-process and independent child-process acquisition for the same path fail, releases it, and confirms acquisition succeeds. A second path alias exists on Windows NTFS through hard links, which bypass lexical/real-path identity; the spool now checks `Stats.nlink` during recovery and before appending, and rejects a shared-link file without changing its bytes. This closes the normal same-host/different-wallet same-path gap without changing wallet identity or paper behavior.

Verification: the final `npm test` passed: core 398, intelligence 551, platform 883, terminal 303 (2,135 total). `npm run build:engine` passed after path canonicalization and hard-link checks. Focused storage tests passed 18/18 and process-lock tests passed 3/3, including separate-process exclusion/release. The hard-link behavior matches Node's documented `Stats.nlink` count and Microsoft's description of paths that share one file. Sources: [Node.js file-system Stats](https://nodejs.org/api/fs.html#statsnlink), [Microsoft hard links](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/fileio/hard-links-and-junctions). SQLite/Node references above support the single-writer and exclusive-bind behavior. No service was restarted and no trading, signing, chain, or capital behavior changed.

Current project/system refresh for this cycle: local `main` and public `origin/main` both remain at `373143a10d05327149cc5d3cbd0cfb44bff9cc18`; the public GitHub repository shows 94 commits, 0 open issues, and 0 open pull requests. The current worktree contains 110 changed/untracked paths, so the generated build outputs and prior user edits were preserved. Read-only requests to local port 8793 returned the paper health endpoint and `/api/live/readiness`: `liveReady:false`, `liveSigningUnavailable:true`, `productionCapitalAuthorityBlocked:true`. The generic health payload did not provide the latter fields, so readiness is taken only from its dedicated response. No process or database was modified.

Remaining: this process lease is a stable hashed TCP port, so a hash collision between unrelated spool paths can cause a safe startup refusal. A hard link created concurrently after the link-count check is outside the normal startup contract, and network filesystems/cross-host coordination are not established. The OS releases the bound port on process death, but file/directory power-loss durability remains the cycle-103 limitation. The package start/check commands, Windows release launcher, and soak runner all enter through `runEngine()`; `--check` exits before spool creation. Next: measure spool occupancy and append latency in existing paper soak telemetry, then preserve the directory-entry power-loss boundary in the release requirements.

## Research cycle 105 — release all runtime leases after partial shutdown failure — 2026-10-06

Reviewed the prior “Upgrades,” “Sol,” “SOL_SYLPH,” and “Solana Simulation Evidence” project chats and the current research blueprint alongside the running local paper readiness endpoint. Rechecked the repository source/build/test state and current public GitHub `main`: local and remote remain at `373143a10d05327149cc5d3cbd0cfb44bff9cc18`, and public issue/PR counts are zero. The read-only readiness route continues to report `liveReady:false`, `liveSigningUnavailable:true`, and `productionCapitalAuthorityBlocked:true`; no runtime, database, or trading policy was changed.

Three concrete reliability findings drove this cycle. First, shutdown cleanup awaited closers sequentially, so a dashboard/store/logger rejection could skip releasing the spool and wallet process leases. Second, spool hard-link detection existed at append and startup recovery, but a link created after startup could still be replaced during successful replay compaction, leaving the alias with stale evidence. Third, the README already requires SQLite to reside on a local filesystem, but `DB_PATH` accepts any string and the process locks coordinate only one host; this leaves a configuration/enforcement gap for a documented deployment constraint. SQLite documents that WAL users must be on one host and that WAL does not work over a network filesystem. The first two were fixed here; the third is an enforcement risk, not a runtime failure demonstrated in this cycle.

Added `closeEngineResources` to attempt each resource close independently, always attempt both lease releases, remove the engine's temporary SIGINT/SIGTERM handlers, and report collected failures only after cleanup attempts. Added a failure-injection regression proving that a rejecting dashboard and logger do not prevent Store close, signal-listener removal, or lease reacquisition. Added a link-count guard at the start of spool compaction, including the empty-record deletion path, and expanded the hard-link test to verify replay refuses to retire the record and both linked paths retain the same bytes. This complements (and does not replace) the existing startup and append checks.

New external primary-source check: Microsoft documents that hard-linked names reference the same file and changes are visible through each path; SQLite's WAL documentation states that participating processes must be on one host and WAL does not work over a network filesystem. Sources: [Microsoft hard links](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/fileio/hard-links-and-junctions), [SQLite WAL](https://www.sqlite.org/wal.html), [Node.js v24 filesystem API](https://nodejs.org/download/release/v24.19.0/docs/api/fs.html). This research validates the hard-link defense and sharpens the unresolved database placement constraint; it does not prove behavior of every network filesystem.

Verification: `npm test` passed before the final narrowly scoped compaction guard with core 399/399, intelligence 551/551, platform 883/883, and terminal 303/303 (2,136 total). The final source then passed `npm run build:engine` and focused spool/runtime-lock tests 22/22, including the new alias-byte-preservation assertion. `git diff --check` returned no whitespace errors (only repository line-ending notices). No full-suite rerun occurred after the last compaction-only change. Existing local edits/generated outputs were preserved; the working tree remains uncommitted.

Remaining: process leases are same-host and a fixed hashed TCP port can safely collide across unrelated paths; the link-count check cannot exclude a malicious link introduced in its check-to-rename window. Parent-directory entry durability after abrupt power loss remains platform/filesystem dependent. `DB_PATH` can point to network storage even though the SQLite WAL contract excludes cross-host/network filesystems; inspect configuration validation and supported deployment surfaces next, then decide whether to reject clearly identifiable remote paths or expose a documented operator constraint without pretending portable mount classification is complete. Next operational research should also measure spool occupancy and append latency in soak telemetry without adding synchronous writes to high-rate feed events. ## Research cycle 106 — reject Windows UNC databases and expose spool health telemetry — 2026-10-06

Continued from cycle 105 using the current worktree and its tracked audit history, including the prior “Upgrades,” “Sol,” “SOL_SYLPH,” and “Solana Simulation Evidence” discoveries. Rechecked the actual configuration, runtime setup path, README recovery instructions, tests, and public repository state. Local `main` and public `origin/main` remain at `373143a10d05327149cc5d3cbd0cfb44bff9cc18`; GitHub reports zero open issues and pull requests. The repository README already says SQLite must be local and each wallet/database must stay on one host. `src/config.ts` nevertheless accepted UNC paths as ordinary strings, even though the main Store and JSONL research spool use WAL-backed persistence and the process lease is a loopback same-host mechanism.

Added platform-aware config validation that rejects Windows UNC database paths, including slash-style UNC and extended `\\?\UNC\...` paths, before startup. It allows ordinary local paths and extended local drive paths. Added regression coverage for those path classes and updated the README to explain what is and is not detected. This narrows an explicit invalid configuration at the primary Engine entry point without claiming portable network-mount detection.

The previous cycle identified that `DurableResearchSpool.getSnapshot()` exposed queue counts to the API but periodic `soak_checkpoint` records omitted spool pressure and append cost. Added bounded in-memory counters for pending payload bytes, configured record/byte ceilings, append count, cumulative append latency, and maximum append latency. The existing periodic checkpoint now includes those scalar values and omits the local spool path; it performs no extra filesystem reads/writes. The append timer surrounds the synchronous durable append call only, excludes rollback and compaction work, and is sampled only when the low-volume spool path actually attempts a disk append. This provides operational measurements, not p95/p99 latency claims; quantiles still require an explicitly bounded histogram or offline analysis of raw samples.

External primary-source comparison: the official SQLite WAL guide says all database users must be on one host because WAL depends on shared memory, and specifically says WAL does not work on a network filesystem. Microsoft documents that Windows UNC names can point to remote shares; these sources support rejecting obvious UNC forms. They do not offer a portable Node API for proving whether a mapped Windows drive or arbitrary POSIX mount is local, so those remain operator-verified deployment conditions. The injected example append measured 0.6424 ms on this machine; this single fixture measurement is not a performance benchmark or p95 estimate. `git diff --check` reported no whitespace errors; only expected Git line-ending notices appeared. Existing uncommitted changes were preserved; nothing was committed and no runtime/database was restarted or mutated.

Remaining: mapped network drives and POSIX network mounts are not classified. A future deployment contract could use an explicit operator-selected storage mode or platform-specific mount inspection, but must not infer local durability solely from a drive letter or successful WAL initialization. ## Research cycle 107 — report bounded spool latency percentiles through the soak UI — 2026-10-06

Continued from cycles 105–106, preserving the prior “Upgrades,” “Sol,” “SOL_SYLPH,” “Solana Simulation Evidence,” and October token-data findings as context rather than re-running completed research. Snapshots and `soak_checkpoint` now expose p50/p95/p99 as **conservative bucket upper bounds**, plus an explicit overflow count. Percentiles falling in the open overflow bucket return `null`; the UI labels that condition as histogram overflow. The histogram is cumulative since process start, retains no samples, and only records actual durable append attempts. Its timer excludes rollback/compaction. It does not claim exact quantiles or interval-window percentiles.

Connected the full telemetry path: spool snapshot → periodic Engine checkpoint → unchanged session JSONL reader → Soak Telemetry UI. The panel shows pending records and payload bytes against configured limits plus p50/p95/p99 append bounds. Missing samples and overflow render explicit text rather than a zero latency. The UI reads only the existing latest checkpoint and does not contact the spool directly. Sources: [HdrHistogramJS project and bucket/percentile API](https://github.com/HdrHistogram/HdrHistogramJS), [HdrHistogram project overview](https://github.com/HdrHistogram/HdrHistogram).

The final full `npm test` passed with core 400/400, intelligence 551/551, platform 884/884, and terminal 304/304 (2,139 total). No real sustained-soak latency distribution was collected in this cycle; example fixture latencies are not a benchmark. No runtime/database was restarted or modified; local edits remain uncommitted.

Remaining: histogram percentiles are process-lifetime cumulative and bucket-conservative; they do not support recent-window regression detection. ## Research cycle 108 — separate checkpoint-window append latency from live cumulative metrics — 2026-10-06

Continued from the cycle-107 open item using the existing Sylph Fusion audit history and prior “Upgrades,” “Sol,” “SOL_SYLPH,” and “Solana Simulation Evidence” project research. The local checkout remains on `main` at the same public baseline SHA (`373143a10d05327149cc5d3cbd0cfb44bff9cc18`); this cycle intentionally preserved the existing broad uncommitted worktree. The key integration review found two consumers of the same spool snapshot: frequent `Engine.snapshot()` dashboard polling and the periodic `soak_checkpoint`. Resetting the original histogram in the shared getter would make UI polling silently erase evidence before persistence.

Added a second fixed-size histogram for checkpoint intervals. Each durable append updates both lifetime and interval counters using the same bucket classification. `getSnapshot()` remains cumulative and read-only; a dedicated checkpoint-only method copies then resets only interval counters and measures elapsed window duration from a monotonic clock. `Engine.emitCheckpoint()` writes the interval alongside the existing cumulative fields, so older readers keep their current fields and newer readers can identify recent-window changes. The Soak Telemetry panel now displays checkpoint-window p50/p95/p99 upper bounds separately from process-lifetime cumulative values, with the same explicit no-samples and histogram-overflow states. Memory remains bounded; raw latency samples are not retained.

External design reference remains the upstream HdrHistogram Recorder API, which exposes interval histograms since the previous retrieval while recording continues. This implementation uses the same separation principle but a small local fixed-bucket counter because writes are already synchronous and serialized on this Node event loop, and the spool has no high-rate multi-threaded recorder. The source motivates interval snapshots; it does not establish this implementation’s performance under real sustained soak. Sources: [HdrHistogram Recorder API](https://hdrhistogram.github.io/HdrHistogram/JavaDoc/org/HdrHistogram/Recorder.html), [HdrHistogramJS](https://github.com/HdrHistogram/HdrHistogramJS).

Verification: full `npm test` passed with core 400/400, intelligence 551/551, platform 884/884, and terminal 304/304 (2,139 total). `npm run build` passed for engine and production UI. Regression coverage proves interval consumption leaves cumulative counters unchanged, an empty later interval is explicit, subsequent samples land in the next interval, dashboard `Engine.snapshot()` cannot consume checkpoint metrics, and the UI identifies the window semantics. `git diff --check` reported no whitespace errors; Git emitted line-ending notices because of the repository’s Windows checkout. Generated build outputs were preserved as the repo’s existing workflow produces them. No commit, push, runtime restart, or database mutation occurred.

Remaining: this is instrumentation, not an operationally measured latency improvement. Run a sustained paper soak and compare checkpoint windows for realistic sample counts, interval durations, and append-tail trends; the currently collected deterministic fixtures do not qualify as benchmarks. The interval updates two fixed bucket arrays per append, which is bounded work but still needs measurement in the actual low-volume audit path. Dashboard currently displays the latest persisted checkpoint interval, while current live snapshot remains cumulative; live rolling windows can be added later only if a consumer requirement justifies another bounded aggregation. Existing deployment risks remain: network mounts/cross-host locking and power-loss directory durability are unresolved; production live readiness remains blocked, and simulation/token datasets remain non-profitability evidence.

Cycle-108 refinement before acceptance: inspection of `emitCheckpoint()` showed the first version consumed interval samples before synchronous session-log persistence returned. The final API now separates non-consuming interval read from explicit reset, and `emitCheckpoint()` resets only after `sessionLogger.writeEvent()` returns successfully. If the write throws, the samples remain for retry. The regression injects that failure and verifies the next successful checkpoint still contains the original sample. Re-ran full `npm test` after this correction (same 400/551/884/304 passing counts), then `npm run build` and targeted `git diff --check`; all passed. This closes the checkpoint write-ordering issue without claiming crash-durable histogram state: process death before the next checkpoint still loses in-memory interval samples, while previously written checkpoint JSONL remains the durable historical record.

The path is canonicalized and common UNC/device network namespaces are rejected before probing. Remote drives are rejected; only removable and fixed media are accepted; unknown, missing, optical, and RAM-disk types fail closed. The probe classifies nested mounted volumes using `GetVolumePathNameW`, rather than trusting a drive-letter prefix.

No mapped network drive existed here, so remote-drive rejection was verified through the injected native result rather than an actual network mount. The user-visible runtime readiness check remains blocked on signing authority and release certification. No database/runtime state was modified.

Sources: [Microsoft `GetVolumePathNameW`](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/api/fileapi/nf-fileapi-getvolumepathnamew), [Microsoft `GetDriveTypeW`](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/api/fileapi/nf-fileapi-getdrivetypew), [SQLite WAL constraints](https://www.sqlite.org/wal.html). ## Research cycle 110 — reject generic Linux FUSE and host-shared filesystems — 2026-10-06

Continued from cycle 109, rechecking the current source, test contract, and repository state before extending the storage policy. Local and public `main` remain at `373143a10d05327149cc5d3cbd0cfb44bff9cc18`; GitHub currently shows zero open issues and zero open pull requests. The broad pre-existing worktree remains intact. The read-only live readiness endpoint remains `liveReady:false`, `liveSigningUnavailable:true`, and `productionCapitalAuthorityBlocked:true`. The project’s “Upgrades,” “Sol,” “SOL_SYLPH,” “Solana Simulation Evidence,” and token-data findings remain research context: none provide a reason to relax the database durability requirement or make profitability claims.

The Linux blocklist still left two ambiguous storage families. The Linux kernel UAPI exposes `FUSE_SUPER_MAGIC` and `HOSTFS_SUPER_MAGIC`; the kernel FUSE documentation describes FUSE as a userspace-provided filesystem whose backing and access behavior are implementation-defined, including use for network filesystems. A generic FUSE identifier therefore cannot prove local, SQLite-compatible locking and durability. `hostfs` explicitly exposes host files through a guest filesystem and is also rejected. The classifier now refuses both types, reporting `FUSE_UNVERIFIED` and `HOSTFS` respectively. The function result for unmatched Linux types is now explicitly `UNCLASSIFIED_FILESYSTEM`; it no longer uses language that could sound like a positive locality finding.

Updated README storage constraints and extended the deterministic filesystem-policy regression to cover both magic values. External primary sources: [Linux kernel filesystem magic identifiers](https://github.com/torvalds/linux/blob/master/include/uapi/linux/magic.h), [Linux kernel FUSE documentation](https://docs.kernel.org/filesystems/fuse/fuse.html), [SQLite WAL requirements](https://www.sqlite.org/wal.html). The evidence supports refusing generic FUSE/hostfs for this WAL deployment; it does not identify the backing type of arbitrary overlay or unknown filesystems.

Verification: the full `npm test` passed after adding FUSE/hostfs rejection: core 400, intelligence 551, platform 889, terminal 304 (2,144 total). The final status-label refinement then passed `npm run build`, focused filesystem-policy tests 5/5 (including this Windows host's native volume probe), and targeted `git diff --check`; only expected Windows line-ending notices appeared. The Windows host's local volume remains classified `DRIVE_FIXED`, no SMB mappings were present in the previous current-state check, and the read-only readiness endpoint remains blocked as above. Linux identifiers were exercised with deterministic injected `statfs` results rather than a Linux-host mount. No database/runtime state changed and no commit/push was made.

Remaining: `overlayfs` can conceal details of its backing store; unknown Linux filesystem types and macOS/non-Linux mounts remain unclassified and still require operator verification. Generic FUSE is intentionally rejected even when a particular FUSE implementation might be local, because this runtime has no adapter-specific proof contract for its locking/durability behavior. Next storage review should determine whether overlayfs and container-mounted paths need explicit classification, without treating a container volume's visible mount name as evidence about its backing store. Sustained soak measurements, directory-entry power-loss durability, and signing/release gates remain open.

## Research cycle 111 — reject unverified Linux OverlayFS and fix Docker database placement — 2026-10-06

Continued the recorded OverlayFS/container storage investigation by tracing the actual startup boundary (`src/fusion.ts` checks the database parent before `new Store`) and the shipped Docker Compose deployment. Re-read the supplied token-data report and project blueprint alongside the earlier “Upgrades,” “Sol,” “SOL_SYLPH,” and “Solana Simulation Evidence” research: the token outcomes remain descriptive, selected/censored, and not evidence of achievable profitability, so they do not justify weakening the local-durability contract. An independent architecture review confirmed that a visible OverlayFS mount cannot establish the writable upper directory's backing-store locality or durability, and found a concrete Compose wiring gap: the deployment mounted `./data:/app/data` but left `DB_PATH` at its project-root default, so the engine database could be placed in the container writable layer.

Linux kernel documentation defines OverlayFS as a merged view over upper and lower directory trees and documents that upper-filesystem and mount options affect durability; the UAPI identifies it as `OVERLAYFS_SUPER_MAGIC` (`0x794c7630`). SQLite WAL requires a same-host filesystem contract. Given the absence of a trusted runtime adapter that traces and validates the overlay upper mount, Linux startup now blocks OverlayFS as `OVERLAYFS_UNVERIFIED` before SQLite opens. Docker Compose now sets `DB_PATH=/app/data/fusion.sqlite`, inside its existing persistent `./data:/app/data` mount. README clarifies that the host-side directory must itself be verified local persistent storage. Added regression coverage for both the filesystem block and the Compose path-to-volume connection. Sources: [Linux OverlayFS documentation](https://docs.kernel.org/filesystems/overlayfs.html), [Linux filesystem magic identifiers](https://github.com/torvalds/linux/blob/master/include/uapi/linux/magic.h), [Docker storage-driver guidance](https://docs.docker.com/engine/storage/drivers/select-storage-driver/), [SQLite WAL requirements](https://www.sqlite.org/wal.html).

Verification: `npm test` passed across the full repository suite (previous cycle baseline 2,144 plus one added Compose-wiring test = 2,145 total); the focused filesystem/Compose contract passed 6/6; `docker compose config --quiet` passed; and targeted `git diff --check` found no whitespace errors (only expected Windows line-ending notices). The current read-only readiness endpoint still reports `liveReady:false`, with `signingAuthority` and `releaseCertification` failing; this storage change does not alter readiness or runtime state. The host is Windows, so Linux magic values were tested through injected `statfs` results rather than a Linux-host mount integration. No commit or push was made.

Remaining: a bind mount's host-side storage can still be remote or ephemeral and is not inspectable from this Compose file; Linux overlay-backed bind mounts and unknown filesystem types remain outside automatic proof. MacOS/non-Linux classification, sustained paper-soak measurements, directory-entry power-loss durability, and signing/release blockers remain open. A future improvement should add a deployable, explicit storage verification command/evidence record or a narrowly supported adapter, rather than treating a bind-mount path as proof of physical durability.

## Research cycle 112 — make terminal network claims match enforced loopback policy — 2026-10-06

Rechecked the just-modified Compose and terminal launch paths, local/public GitHub state, and the current runtime gate. Local and public `main` are still `373143a10d05327149cc5d3cbd0cfb44bff9cc18`; GitHub reports zero open issues and zero open PRs. The worktree now has 126 changed/untracked paths, including existing project work; none were discarded. The local read-only readiness endpoint remains `liveReady:false`, `liveSigningUnavailable:true`, `productionCapitalAuthorityBlocked:true`; `signingAuthority` fails because no isolated signer is configured and `releaseCertification` fails because no certified release root exists.

Architecture review compared the Docker path end-to-end. Compose publishes host loopback `127.0.0.1:8793` to the container port, while `terminal/server.mjs` hardcodes a loopback bind and `isLocalRequest()` also requires a loopback socket peer. The container healthcheck curls container loopback, so its success does not prove host-published reachability. The runner previously printed “Remote Operator: ENABLED,” selected a `0.0.0.0` host by default, and set `ALLOW_REMOTE_OPERATOR=true`; the terminal ignores those settings and continues enforcing loopback-only access. Docker's port publishing forwards traffic to the container's listening port; host-only publishing does not make a listener bound to container loopback reachable. A blanket bind-to-all change or removal of the peer check would expand the security boundary, and behavior differs between Docker Desktop and native Linux. Primary references: [Docker port publishing](https://docs.docker.com/engine/network/port-publishing/), [Docker Desktop networking](https://docs.docker.com/desktop/features/networking/), [Compose published-port host IP](https://docs.docker.com/reference/compose-file/services/), [Docker host networking limitations](https://docs.docker.com/engine/network/drivers/host/).

Corrected the runner's emitted bind/access status to say loopback/local-only, removed its `--host` override, and force-set the ignored remote-access flag false. Removed the misleading `TERMINAL_HOST` entries from the Dockerfile and Compose environment. Added a regression assertion that the runner reports the terminal's actual loopback-only policy and does not claim remote access. This is a truthful status/security-boundary correction, not a Docker host-reachability fix. `node --test terminal/test/local-bind.test.mjs` passed 3/3; `docker compose config --quiet` passed; targeted `git diff --check` found no whitespace errors beyond expected line-ending notices. The Docker CLI can parse the Compose file, but `docker compose ps` failed because the Docker Desktop Linux daemon pipe is absent. Consequently, no live-container host reachability, request-peer identity, or cross-platform isolation result is verified in this environment.

Remaining: the shipped Compose port mapping is likely unusable from the host under the app's strict loopback peer check, and the container has no live integration proof. There is no portable Compose-only fix that is supported by current evidence while retaining the peer guard. A dedicated localhost-bound host proxy with an authenticated, browser-compatible session boundary is a candidate architecture; alternatively, use the standalone host launcher for local UI access. Any Docker UI rework needs a separately reviewed network/auth design and live tests on Docker Desktop and native Linux, including host requests, non-loopback peer rejection, direct container-IP access, and a second host. Do not interpret the green container healthcheck as proof of published-port reachability.

## Research cycle 115 — correct calibration metrics and make empty evidence explicit — 2026-10-06

The new token report recommends calibration against outcomes on later untouched periods and warns that correlated tokens, sample selection, variable observation windows, missing entry quotes, and unverified publisher fills make naive counts overconfident. While tracing the project's existing calibration path, `BayesBeliefEngine.evaluateCalibration` was found to return mean absolute per-record forecast error under the `expected_calibration_error` name. That is not ECE: ECE compares each prediction bin's mean probability with its observed event frequency and weights the absolute gap by that bin's population. The method also fabricated low scores (`Brier 0.12`, `ECE 0.05`) for zero observations and labeled any large ECE as “overconfident,” even when forecasts were underconfident.

The implementation now calculates a fixed-width 10-bin ECE, preserves the signed aggregate mean calibration gap to distinguish overprediction, validates probabilities and binary outcomes without JSON coercion, and returns `INSUFFICIENT_DATA` with null metrics and null overconfidence when the sample is empty. This corrects a diagnostic only; it does not attach execution provenance to labels, account for clustered token/time dependence, supply confidence intervals, or make calibration a live authorization.

The implementation follows the probability/frequency comparison documented by [scikit-learn's calibration curves](https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/calibration.html) and [CalibrationDisplay](https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.calibration.CalibrationDisplay.html). Binned ECE remains descriptive and sensitive to bin choice; a recent primary research preprint, [EDGE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20511), likewise describes binned ECE as lacking a null distribution and depending on binning. No inferential guarantee is claimed by this code.

Added four focused regressions for the binned-vs-record-level distinction, empty input, malformed probability/label rejection, and perfect calibration. `npm run build:engine` and the focused calibration tests passed 7/7; the final full `npm test` passed core 401, intelligence 555, platform 890, and terminal 306 (2,152 total). The GitHub main SHA, readiness failures, and unavailable Docker daemon remain as recorded in cycle 114. No commit or push was made.

Remaining: the calibrator still accepts outcomes without source-quality, fill, censoring, or cohort metadata, so a caller could misuse retrospective peaks as binary labels. Before any probability influences trading, add an outcome contract tied to decision-time information and execution-quality states, retain censored/failed cases explicitly, and validate on later untouched time cohorts with dependence-aware uncertainty. Do not interpret a green Brier/ECE diagnostic as proof of profitable or executable predictions.

## Research cycle 116 — stop fabricating Einstein reference-frame telemetry — 2026-10-06

Following the token report's warning that observed maxima and sampled price paths are not executable returns, traced Einstein metrics from `MasterIntelligenceEngine` into the terminal inspector. The orchestrator supplied fabricated inputs (`volume = liquidity × 1.5`, fixed token/SOL one-hour returns, and fixed slippage), and the inspector supplied optimistic defaults for missing values and mislabeled a normalized heuristic as a statistical z-score. These could make absent observations look like measured market evidence. Jupiter's current documentation has also moved to Swap API V2; its V1 quote page says V1 is superseded and defines quotes around raw input amount and slippage thresholds. This repo change does not add a Jupiter quote adapter and does not treat a route identifier alone as executable evidence. Sources: [Jupiter Swap API V2](https://developers.jup.ag/docs/swap), [Jupiter V1 quote semantics and deprecation](https://developers.jup.ag/docs/swap/v1/get-quote).

Einstein reference-frame inputs now use an explicit observed/unavailable contract. Volume requires a nonnegative observation with source identity and a valid interval no later than decision time; relative return requires matched one-hour USD observations and computes the ratio of gross growth factors; slippage requires a bounded basis-point quote bound to an integer lamport trade size, route, observation identity, and validity window. The orchestrator forwards only supplied observations. The UI now presents missing values as unavailable, keeps actual relative return separate from its normalized heuristic score, identifies the Bayesian estimate as uncalibrated, and removes the unsupported external-organic claim. Other fabricated/synthetic inputs elsewhere in the broad orchestrator/UI remain outside this focused repair and need their own source tracing.

## Research cycle 119 — preserve Jupiter quote time and blockhash expiry

Continued from the V2 migration by auditing how the result flows into `Executor.build` and its pending-order metadata. The V2 adapter had been discarding `blockhashWithMetadata` and returning the older pool snapshot's timestamp as the quote time. The snapshot TTL was checked only before the awaited network request; an API/ALT lookup taking longer than the TTL could still produce a paper build. Jupiter's current Router Build docs identify `blockhashWithMetadata` as a response field and use its `lastValidBlockHeight` for transaction confirmation; they also distinguish V0 ALT responses from transaction-version-one responses. The adapter requests the default V0 path, validates the 32-byte blockhash payload and positive safe-integer last-valid height, stamps quote receipt time, carries the last-valid height into the paper pending record, and rechecks source-snapshot freshness after route plus ALT awaits. Source: [Jupiter V2 Build response and expiry contract](https://developers.jup.ag/docs/swap/build).

Added regressions for quote timestamp/last-valid-height propagation, absent or malformed expiry metadata, and a delayed V2 route that crosses the snapshot TTL and must reject. Focused Jupiter/failure-injection tests passed 15/15. The final full `npm test` passed core 407, intelligence 561, platform 890, terminal 306 (2,164 total); TypeScript engine build passed. The real local `.env` continues to point to V2 but contains no API key, so the real-provider path still fails before network access; no network call was made. The running service was not restarted. Live readiness remained 8/10 passing (`signingAuthority`, `releaseCertification` fail) when last sampled in cycle 118. No commit/push; unrelated worktree changes remain preserved.

Remaining: tests use a documentation-shaped fixture rather than a captured provider response, and no real Jupiter credential was available. Keep all submissions quarantined pending reviewed transaction assembly, simulation, exact fee accounting, and end-to-end reconciliation.

## Research cycle 120 — reconcile Jupiter minimum output with paper proceeds

Continued the V2 contract audit through `Executor.graduatedSell`, `Executor.build`, paper cash settlement, and `buildOutcomeLabel`. The output mint is the native-SOL wrapped mint, so provider raw output is lamports. Jupiter's official V2 Build docs define fixed `slippageBps`, and Jupiter's official slippage guide says the estimated slippage is reflected in `otherAmountThreshold`; the published API schema describes that threshold as the minimum output after applying slippage to `outAmount`. This supports using `outAmount` for gross quoted output and the exact threshold for conservative paper settlement, not treating the quote as an observed fill. Sources: [Jupiter V2 Build](https://developers.jup.ag/docs/swap/build), [Jupiter slippage estimation](https://github.com/jup-ag/docs/blob/main/swap/advanced/slippage.mdx), [Jupiter quote API schema](https://github.com/jup-ag/jupiter-quote-api-node/blob/main/swagger.yaml).

The prior adapter accepted a provider minimum output that was stricter than the requested fixed bound, then discarded it; `build()` recomputed its own lower bound from `outAmount`. `graduatedSell` now requires the exact integer-rounded fixed-slippage threshold and returns it explicitly; the graduated paper sell settles at that minimum. The existing bonding-curve path keeps its own haircut. Outcome labels previously received the already haircut-adjusted cash proceeds and then subtracted the same haircut again as `dexImpactLamports`; the paper exit label now records the threshold proceeds with zero additional impact deduction and carries the modeled haircut/proceeds basis as research metadata. Added tests for exact threshold validation/carry and for avoiding duplicate modeled slippage deduction.

No real provider request was made; the fixture is documentation-shaped, not captured from Jupiter. No signing, submission, persistence migration, commit, or push was performed. ## Research cycle 122 — connect private simulation receipts to canonical CPI normalization — 2026-10-06

Audited the current private devnet unsigned-simulation observer and the existing `cpi-trace-normalizer`. The observer already requested `innerInstructions: true` and validated program IDs, account addresses, stack-height ranges, field sizes, group indexes, and response bytes, but independently reduced the reported trace to a raw JSON hash and labeled any trace with known stack heights `COMPLETE`. That bypassed the repository's bounded canonical normalizer, which checks ordered sparse groups, legal CPI stack paths, parent resolution, source-field unions, cycle/accessor safety and canonical trace digests. The external Solana RPC JSON contract confirms simulation's inner-instruction field may be null/omitted when unavailable and that reported inner instructions are parsed/partially decoded, so absence cannot be treated as an empty proven trace. Sources: [Solana `simulateTransaction`](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/http/simulatetransaction), [Solana RPC JSON structures](https://solana.com/docs/rpc/json-structures), [Solana transaction introspection](https://solana.com/docs/core/transactions/transaction-introspection).

Connected `normalizeSimulationCpiTrace` directly inside the existing private observer, after the response is bound to a one-shot disclosure permit and validated. Receipt metadata now uses the normalized canonical trace hash and reports `STRUCTURALLY_VALID`, `PARTIAL`, or `UNAVAILABLE`; structurally valid deliberately describes reported nodes only and does not assert trace completeness. Malformed and impossible stack paths now fail closed. Added observer regressions for impossible CPI stack paths while preserving missing/null/partial behavior. This keeps the integration non-authorizing and within the observer's existing private boundary.
